5 DOWNSTREAM PNM MEASUREMENTS
5.1     Downstream Transmit and Received Power
5.1.1     Overview
This section describes DOCSIS 3.1 OFDM downstream power measurement for CMTS 3 transmit and CM receive
and how the measurement differs compared to legacy 6-MHz- or 8-MHz-wide SC-QAM signals.

5.1.2     Background
[SCTE Measurement] defines digital signal power (also called digital channel power) as
      "…the power level as measured by a power meter which uses a thermocouple as a transducer. That is, the
      measurement is the average power in the signal, integrated over the actual occupied bandwidth of that signal.
      The signal power is normally expressed in decibels with respect to one millivolt rms in a 75Ω system (dBmV).
      Thus, the measurement reported is the rms value of the sinusoid that would produce the same heating in a 75Ω
      resistor as does the actual signal." (p. 17)
From the above definition, digital channel power for downstream SC-QAM signals carried in cable networks is the
average power in the signal's 6-MHz occupied bandwidth (8 MHz in Europe).
This definition does not apply to a DOCSIS 3.1 downstream OFDM signal because the signal's occupied bandwidth
can vary from 24 MHz to 192 MHz. [PHYv3.1] defines OFDM power as the power per CTA channel, which provides
RF signal level information comparable to SC-QAM digital channel power, i.e., the average power per 6 MHz.
[CTA-542-D] defines the frequencies for the 6-MHz-wide channels used in cable television networks.

5.1.3     OFDM Power at the CMTS
The first part of this section is taken from [PHYv3.1], Section 7.5.9.1, "CMTS Output Electrical Requirements."
      For OFDM, all modulated subcarriers in an OFDM channel are set to the same average power (except pilots
      which are boosted by 6 dB). For purposes of spurious emissions requirements, the "commanded transmit power
      per channel" for an equivalent legacy DOCSIS channel is computed as follows:
          •    CMTS power is configured by power per [CTA] channel and number of occupied [CTA] channels for
               each OFDM channel.
          •    For each OFDM channel, the total power is Power per [CTA] channel + 10log10 (Number of occupied
               [CTA] channels) for that OFDM channel.
          •    CMTS calculates power for data subcarrier and pilots (using total number of non-zero valued (non-
               excluded) subcarriers).
          •    CMTS calculates power in [the] 6 MHz [encompassed spectrum] containing [the] PLC.
          •    For the spurious emissions requirements, power calculated for the 6 MHz containing the PLC is the
               commanded average power of an equivalent DOCSIS legacy channel for that OFDM channel.
      A CMTS MUST output an OFDM RF modulated signal with the characteristics defined in Table 41 - RF
      Output Electrical Requirements, Table 42 - CMTS Output Power, and Table 43 - CMTS Output Out-of-Band
      Noise and Spurious Emissions Requirements [PHYv3.1]. Legacy DOCSIS RF modulated signal characteristics
      are provided in Section 6.2.22.
The CMTS configuration for RF output level will be equivalent for an OFDM signal and an SC-QAM (ITU-T J.83
Annex B) signal because both are based on 6 MHz occupied bandwidths.
The required power per channel for Neq' channels combined onto a single RF port is
                                          Power = 60 – ceil [3.6*log2(N*)] dBmV


3 Here, CMTS refers to integrated or distributed architecture systems.




09/27/23                                            CableLabs                                                     33
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 34 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                             CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


Note the use of the ceiling (ceil) function and base 2 logarithm in the above formula. 4
For more information about CMTS output power, see [PHYv3.1], Section 7.5.9.1.1, "Power per Channel for CMTS,"
Table 42 in particular.

5.1.4    Setting OFDM Channel Power at the CMTS or CCAP
In most cases, OFDM channel power is set to the same power spectral density (PSD) as SC-QAM signals. 5 In other
words, the heights of the OFDM and SC-QAM "haystacks" as seen on a spectrum analyzer are the same (Figure 2).




                   Figure 2 - Example of Same PSD Used for OFDM Signal and SC-QAM Signals


5.1.5    RF Input to the Cable Modem
The following RF signal level parameters for the cable modem input are from [PHYv3.1].
    •    Total input power less than 40 dBmV, 54 MHz to 1.794 GHz (negligible input power outside this frequency
         range)
    •    Level range equal to -9 dBmV to +21 dBmV (in 24-MHz occupied bandwidth), which is equivalent PSD to
         -15 dBmV to +15 dBmV per 6-MHz-wide SC-QAM signal

5.1.6    Downstream Power Measurement on a Spectrum Analyzer
When looking at the OFDM signal on a conventional spectrum analyzer with a typical resolution bandwidth (RBW)
filter setting of 300 kHz, a correction factor needs to be calculated to get the power in a 6-MHz-equivalent
bandwidth and, if desired, for total power.
For example, assume a spectrum analyzer power cursor reads 35 dBmV. This value is the power in the analyzer's
300-kHz RBW. The correction factor used to find the power in the 6-MHz bandwidth is 10*log10(6/0.3), or 13 dB.
The correction factor is added to the power level from the analyzer to find the actual power equivalent for 6 MHz.
                                               35 dBmV + 13 dB = 48 dBmV
If the OFDM signal is flat, the total power of a 192-MHz-wide OFDM signal is found by this equation.
                                          38 dBmV + 10*log10(192/6) = 53 dBmV




4 The ceiling function is a mathematical function that returns the lowest valued integer that is greater than or equal to a given

value. For example, ceil[32.1] = 33, but ceil[32] = 32.
5 Some operators transmit the downstream OFDM signal at a higher PSD than SC-QAM signals to increase headroom for higher

modulation orders such as 4096-QAM. Caution is urged because of the potential to increase downstream total power enough to cause
laser clipping and/or amplifier (and possibly CPE) compression. Extensive lab and field testing beforehand is recommended.


09/27/23                                           CableLabs                                                                 34
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 35 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                    CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


This value can be useful information for laser loading concerns. The shape of the RBW filter can affect power
measurements at the edge of the OFDM band or in the presence of exclusion bands, and the calculation of total power
will be affected by a non-flat (e.g., tilted) OFDM signal.
In some cases, it might be preferable, or easier, to measure OFDM power with a dedicated DOCSIS 3.1 test instrument
or a DOCSIS 3.1 cable modem (or modem-equipped field meter). Some test instruments are also capable of measuring
total power within a specified bandwidth, such as that of an entire OFDM signal; however, be aware of the absolute
accuracy limitations of the device being used for the power measurement.
NOTE: What about OFDM power measurements in other than a 6-MHz equivalent? In areas that use Euro-DOCSIS
      and 8-MHz-wide SC-QAM signals, confusion about OFDM channel power can occur. Although [PHYv3.1]
      defines OFDM power as the power per CTA channel (i.e., power per 6 MHz), some operators might prefer
      the equivalent power per 8 MHz. OFDM channel power per 6 MHz reported by a DOCSIS 3.1 device can be
      converted to an 8-MHz equivalent by taking into account a correction factor added to the 6 MHz power equal
      to 10*log10(8 MHz/6 MHz), or 1.25 dB. In the above example of 38 dBmV (power per 6 MHz), the addition of
      the correction factor would give a channel factor of 39.25 dBmV. The total power would, of course, be the
      same as before, taking into account that the number of 8-MHz-wide channels occupied by a 192-MHz-wide
      OFDM signal is 24: 39.25 dBmV + 10*log10(24) = 53 dBmV.

5.1.7   Downstream Power Measurement on Other Instruments
In addition to a conventional spectrum analyzer, other types of test equipment can be used to measure downstream
OFDM power. At the high end are laboratory-grade vector signal analyzers and DOCSIS analyzers. Additionally,
several test equipment vendors have developed DOCSIS 3.1 field meters and similar instruments, most of which
include an embedded DOCSIS 3.1 cable modem to support a variety of OFDM measurements (channel power,
RxMER per subcarrier, codeword errors, etc.). Here, too, be aware of the absolute accuracy limitations of the device
being used for the power measurement.
As mentioned, OFDM channel bandwidth (occupied bandwidth) can vary from a minimum of 24 MHz (minimum
modulated spectrum of 22 MHz) to a maximum of 192 MHz (maximum encompassed spectrum of 190 MHz). In the
channel lineup in Figure 3, a 96-MHz-wide OFDM signal includes a 1-MHz guard band (GB) on each end of the
channel so that the encompassed spectrum is 94 MHz with 1,880 subcarriers at 50 kHz per subcarrier. The screen
shots in Figure 3 and Figure 4 are from a field meter equipped with a DOCSIS 3.1 CM.




 Figure 3 - Downstream Channel Lineup with a 96-MHz-Wide OFDM Signal at the Upper End of the Spectrum


In the case of the OFDM signal shown in Figure 3, the number of occupied CTA channels is 96 MHz/6 MHz, or
16 channels. Figure 4 shows the OFDM power for that OFDM signal, with the power per 6 MHz represented by
multiple adjacent horizontal lines whose heights represent the average power in each 6-MHz-wide CTA channel
slot.




09/27/23                                      CableLabs                                                          35
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 36 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                  CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927




    Figure 4 - Power Measurements for a 96-MHz-Wide OFDM Channel as 16 Occupied Channels in 6-MHz
         Segments and for the Lower and Upper Adjacent SC-QAM Channel at Left and Right Edges


As defined in [PHYv3.1], the OFDM signal level at the CM input should be between -15 dBmV and +15 dBmV
(power per 6 MHz). The example in Figure 4 meets those requirements with the following measurements: average,
-3.3 dBmV; minimum, -5.3 dBmV; and maximum, -2.7 dBmV.
An important point when looking at OFDM power as shown in Figure 4 is that the lower and upper edges of the
OFDM signal might not fully occupy their respective CTA channel slots even though those slots are considered
"occupied" by the OFDM signal. If one considers each 6-MHz CTA channel occupied by the OFDM signal as a "bin"
containing power data, the edge bins could have less power than the other bins between the edges. In other words,
the 6-MHz slots on each end of the OFDM signal could show lower power than the fully occupied 6-MHz slots.

5.1.8     Impact of Pilots on OFDM Power
Downstream OFDM continuous and scattered pilots are boosted by 6 dB per [PHYv3.1]. Figure 5 shows an example
of a 192-MHz-wide downstream OFDM signal where the boosted continuous pilots are visible. There is some question
as to whether pilot boosting affects OFDM channel power, but the presence of continuous and scattered pilots in an
OFDM signal affects the signal's total power by less than 1 dB.




        Figure 5 - Spectrum Analyzer Display of an OFDM Signal Showing the Boosted Continuous Pilots


5.1.9     Implementation
Methods for polling these data elements from cable modems are given in [CM-OSSIv3.1]. Additional details are
available from CableLabs in the Common Collection Framework.

5.1.10 Conclusions and Future Work
Downstream transmit and received power levels are basic, important measurements for a stable service. However,
differences among measurement devices and conditions must be carefully considered when interpreting these
measurements. For more information, see [SCTE 257]. Also, different CMTS, CCAP, and CM vendors should be
compared to note any differences in specification interpretation.




09/27/23                                     CableLabs                                                          36
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 37 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                     CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


5.2     Downstream Wideband Spectrum Analysis
5.2.1    Overview
The PNM Best Practices document for DOCSIS 3.0 technology [PNMP-3.0] includes full-band capture (FBC), which
is possibly one of the most useful features available in PNM. FBC allows operators to quickly capture and analyze
the downstream RF spectrum from the location and perspective of the cable modem. This powerful tool, the equivalent
of a spectrum analyzer in every home with an FBC-capable modem, allows remote RF power measurement without
deploying a technician or requiring access to the cable modem's physical location. Section 7 of [PNMP-3.0] describes
FBC in detail; this section focuses on new information specific to DOCSIS 3.1 technology and additional
improvements made since [PNMP-3.0].

5.2.2    Description of Measurement
Downstream wideband spectrum analysis in a CM measures the RF at the receiver front-end without relying on the
demodulators (see Figure 6). This measurement is taken by sampling RF in the time domain with an analog-to-digital
converter (ADC) and then converting to the frequency domain using fast Fourier transform (FFT). The resulting
measurement is stored as logarithmic magnitude for each frequency bin of the given center frequency and the
resolution bandwidth specified in the MIB configuration parameters listed in Table 58 of [CM-OSSIv3.1].




                               Figure 6 - Digital Spectrum Analyzer Block Diagram


5.2.3    Capabilities and Limitations
Though the ability to capture and report wideband RF spectrum is a powerful tool for cable operators to analyze
their networks, there are limitations when compared to traditional field instruments. One significant limitation of
FBC is that the magnitude-only measurement lacks important information about the phase component of the signal.
Without knowing the phase component, analysis is limited to power measurement, which precludes operators from
taking signal quality measurements such as RxMER. Because of this, some impairments such as low-level ingress
cannot be detected because the interference level does not exceed the peak-to-average power ratio of the signal. In
cases where the interference is significant, this technique works only in extreme interference levels (see Figure 8).
Other limitations are based on FBC's capture speed and dynamic range, which makes it unsuitable for certain
troubleshooting use cases. FBC has a relatively slow capture rate of several samples per second; therefore, certain
types of interference such as fast, bursty noise can be difficult or impossible to detect. Additionally, with the
wideband front-end, dynamic range typically falls between 30 dB and 40 dB depending on implementation. This
range generally works well for signal power measurement, but it does have limitations when the capture window
power variation exceeds the dynamic range. In the example shown in Figure 7, the dynamic range limits can be seen
at 800 MHz and 880 MHz as artificial power steps in the noise floor. This effect is caused by the front-end gain
controller automatically adjusting to maximize the signal fidelity (see Figure 9) at the expense of noise floor
accuracy. Note that because full-band capture is a compilation of many individual frequency captures over time,
discontinuities can occur that are not necessarily attributable to network impairments.
The example spectrum shown in Figure 7 includes 6-MHz-wide SC-QAM, analog pilots, and OFDM signals. The
screen capture in Figure 8 is from the same node but shows that the location has a shielding integrity problem. Note
the FM, VHF, UHF, LTE, and GSM interference.




09/27/23                                       CableLabs                                                         37
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 38 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                         CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927




                  Figure 7 - Typical Downstream Wideband Spectrum Analysis of RF Spectrum




         Figure 8 - Downstream Wideband Spectrum Analysis of RF Spectrum, Including Interference


In Figure 9, an artificial power step, the result of the dynamic range limitation of this device, is visible in the noise
floor at 800 MHz. The actual noise floor is much lower at 900 MHz, but it cannot be acquired because of the
presence of signal power that exceeds the dynamic range within the capture window.




              Figure 9 - Dynamic Range Limitations of Downstream Wideband Spectrum Analysis



09/27/23                                         CableLabs                                                             38
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 39 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                         CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


Another limitation of wideband spectrum capture is that it is constrained to the downstream spectrum available to the
receiver. This constraint is a function of the filtering designed to protect the CM receiver from upstream burst energy.
However, as seen in Figure 10, the upstream band is still available to the FFT and does produce a spectrum capture
that is subject to the diplexer filter's rejection. Noise can sometimes be observed through, around, and near the
diplexer's rejection area. This technique can be used to detect upstream noise by inference rather than direct observation.




                           Figure 10 - Upstream Spectrum and Diplexer Filter Rejection


5.2.4    Averaging
As with most spectrum analyzers, averaging typically improves the accuracy of power measurement. However, there
are many ways to implement averaging and many different things to consider, such as capture speed and number of
samples. In the case of PNM, the MIB specifies an averaging function for the wideband spectrum capture engine,
going so far as to describe a uniform technique (the "leaky integrator" method), though the results when implemented
in the field are less than uniform. Figure 11 illustrates this point by comparing the results of two DOCSIS SoC
(system on a chip) manufacturers' implementations captured on the same RF plant. Both samples used the maximum
averaging allowed for their respective implementations.




               Figure 11 - Maximum On-Chip Averaging Achieved with Different Implementations




09/27/23                                         CableLabs                                                             39
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 40 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                          CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


Given the differences in implementations, accommodations must be made in resolution bandwidth and the use of
external averaging to improve detection consistency. External software applications are sometimes required to
average multiple samples.
For power to be averaged correctly, the amplitude bins need to be converted from log decibels to linear power. The
same frequency, resolution, and windowing parameters need to be used when collecting samples, then the samples
are summed and divided as linear power and converted back to log decibels. This operation must be done bin by bin
using common bin center frequencies.
See Sections 11.8.5 and 11.8.6 of SCTE 270 2021r1 for converting between dBmV and watts.

5.2.5    Channel Power Accuracy and Calibration
Due to variations in the component chain of the RF tuners, power calibration needs to be used to maximize the power
accuracy of information reported by the DOCSIS MIBs. In most contemporary DOCSIS tuner implementations, the
power levels are measured against a known source, and a calibration (power offset) value is stored in an internal
table for future reference. Because the wideband spectrum measurement is taken ahead of the tuner and demodulator,
it is not subject to equalization or power correction with the internal calibration tables; therefore, the power
measurements for a given spectrum bandwidth will generally not match the total channel power measured by the
tuner and reported in the channel power MIBs.
The potential for mismatched information reported between the DOCSIS power and PNM wideband spectrum capture
MIBs can be a problem for the people and systems responsible for interpreting the information. One technique for
overcoming the reporting discrepancy is to compare the channel power measured through wideband capture with the
power measured by the CM tuner. The power differential measured within each of the tuner channels can be averaged
to produce a calibration offset value. This technique essentially reveals the internal calibration offset, which can then
be applied to the power measurements obtained by the wideband power measurement. Depending on the component
chain variation, this calibration can vary significantly; using it is strongly advised to avoid confusion.
Calibration begins by using three DOCS-IF MIBs to acquire the calibrated channel receiver power from the DOCSIS
downstream tuners: docsIfDownChannelFrequency, docsIfDownChannelPower, and docsIfDownChannelWidth. In
the case of a relatively flat RF spectrum, a simple strategy can be to create an average of all downstream receive
power values. All averaging needs to be done in linear power; therefore, the log decibel millivolt (dBmV) values
need to be converted to linear, averaged, and then converted back to log decibel millivolt. In the example shown in
Table 1, the average receive power across all channels is -0.1 dBmV.
The next step is to instruct the spectrum capture MIB to measure the same channel center frequency and RF bandwidth
by setting FirstSegmentCenterFrequency, LastSegmentCenterFrequency, and SegmentFrequencySpan to produce the
desired channelization. In the example shown in Table 1, these values would be 603000000, 645000000, and 6000000,
respectively. After enabling the PNM MIB, the resulting BinAmplitudeFileData will return the TotalSegmentPower
measurement using the wideband capture MIB. These values can be averaged in a manner similar to the previous
step by using the DOCSIS tuner power measurement. The example shown in Table 2 yields an average channel
power of 3.04 dBmV across the same eight channels of RF spectrum. Note that the values in the NumberOfAverages
parameter of the calibration routine should match the values used for regular power measurement to minimize
inconsistencies. In general, greater averaging results in more accurate (useful) power measurements of wideband
signals.
Finally, the average channel powers are subtracted to produce a single offset value that can be applied to all of the
channels measured using the wideband spectrum analysis engine. In this example, 3.04 dBmV minus 0.1 dB produces
an offset value of 2.94 dB, which can then be applied to channels of similar spectrum bandwidth. Likewise, the
2.94 dBmV can be converted to different equivalent power bandwidths and used to calibrate any variation of
resolution bandwidth power measurements.

           Table 1 - Example of Downstream DOCSIS Channel Center Frequency and Receive Power

Center Frequency (MHz)                    603        609       615       621       627        633       639       645

Channel Receive Power (dBmV)               0.1       -0.2       0        0.2        0.1        0        -0.2      -0.1

Channel Width (MHz)                         6         6         6         6          6         6         6         6




09/27/23                                         CableLabs                                                              40
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 41 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                         CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


           Table 2 - Example of Wideband Spectrum Channel Center Frequency and Receive Power

Center Frequency (MHz)                    603       609       615        621       627       633       639       645

Channel Receive Power (dBmV)              3.3        3.0       3.0       3.1       3.0       3.2       2.9       2.8

Channel Width (MHz)                        6         6          6         6         6         6         6         6



This method of calibration has been demonstrated at scale to produce significant improvements in power accuracy
when compared to commercial-grade field measurement tools (see Figure 12). However, the achievable performance
of these tools may differ depending on total input power, temperature, and filter design, among other factors. If
maximum power level accuracy is a requirement, then specific makes and models of cable modems (including
firmware versions) should be characterized for a full understanding of the limitations under these different conditions.




    Figure 12 - Power Level Comparison of Field Gear and Wideband Spectrum Analysis After Calibration
                          (Figure Note: Individual product names have been removed.)


5.2.6    Spectral Impairment Detection
The ultimate goal of wideband spectral analysis is to proactively detect network impairments before they have an
impact on tuner and demodulator performance. As stated earlier, having the full RF spectrum provides a larger
context for troubleshooting beyond the relatively narrow bandwidth observed by the downstream tuners and signal
demodulators. For example, many common impairments such as amplitude ripple—commonly but incorrectly called
standing waves (see Figure 13)—often cannot be measured within the bandwidth of a single 6-MHz-wide SC-QAM
channel. However, having the full spectrum bandwidth of the wideband analyzer improves the ability to perceive
these types of problems. In the example of a standing wave, knowing the peak-to-peak or null-to-null frequency
periodicity of the wave is a useful tool for understanding fault distances of echo cavities.
As an adjunct project for the CableLabs PNM working group, some of the members have collaborated on software
libraries that automatically detect common RF impairments. This software, known as the spectral impairment detector,
was written in the Java programming language and is available to NDA members. Many of the following impairments
and functions are included, with a brief description of each.




09/27/23                                        CableLabs                                                             41
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 42 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                         CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927




                             Figure 13 - Examples of Typical RF Spectral Impairments


5.2.6.1    Spectral Impairment Detector Channelization
The spectral impairment detector algorithms are highly dependent on analysis of certain channel properties such as
tilt, power, and frequency. To facilitate this analysis, an important function of the library is to channelize the
FBC amplitude bin using one of several available methods. The channelization functions are contained in the
PNMSpectrumChannelFactory and PNMSpectrumTuner software objects, which, notably, do not actually tune the
RF spectrum. Because FBC lacks a sufficiently fast capture speed (symbol rate) and is limited to log magnitude
bins, true tuning is impossible. The library does, however, provide several tuner-like capabilities that are important
to the subsequent function of the spectral impairment detector software.
Predefined channel maps and blind scanning are the two primary methods used by spectral impairment detector to
convert amplitude versus frequency bins to usable channel information. The first method is the most common
because most operators and users of the software will generally have this information available. The minimum
information required includes the center frequencies of known channels within the RF lineup. It is also possible to
provide discrete programming information for each of the RF channels, which is helpful when troubleshooting
customer-reported issues. The PNMSpectrumTuner object may also be invoked with a standard U.S. or European
channel plan, which contains a list of well-known channel center frequencies and widths. Either method results in a
list of channel objects, which provides useful information beyond the simple amplitude bins.

5.2.6.2    Spectral Impairment Detector Channel Measurements
Channelizing the amplitude versus frequency bins makes the channel information more meaningful for analysis.
The tuner library contains several software types within the channelization, including PNMDocsisChannel,
PNMSpectrumCATVChannel, and PNMSpectrumChannel, which expose more useful attributes for use in analysis.
Some of these attributes are listed below.
    •     Start, center, and end frequency—the respective frequencies of the channel in hertz
    •     Name—a user-friendly name for the RF channel that may be displayed in user interfaces
    •     Width—the channel bandwidth specified in hertz
    •     CTA—(formerly EIA and CEA) the industry standard identifier for a given channel and frequency that
          refers to a 6-MHz channel plan (Europe uses an 8-MHz plan) 6



6 The 6-MHz channel plan for cable networks can be found in the Consumer Technology Association standard CTA-542-D R-2018.




09/27/23                                         CableLabs                                                             42
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 43 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                           CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


    •     ICFR—the in-channel frequency response measured as the minimum and maximum within the symbol rate
          bandwidth (for example, 5.12 MHz for a 6.4-MHz-wide US channel)
    •     Tilt start and end levels—the power levels of the leading and trailing bins within the channel
    •     Median dB—the median power value calculated from the median of the channel's bins
    •     Ripples—calculated as the number of incidents in which a bin power transition has occurred within a given
          threshold
    •     Tilt—calculated as the difference between the tilt start and tilt end values; results may be positive or negative
    •     MER/SNR—known only for DOCSIS channels when calibrated using the tuner MIB values
    •     Bin pointers—useful indices that point to the respective channel locations within the main bin array

5.2.6.3    Adjacency and Channel Alignment
Adjacency misalignment is an RF impairment recognizable as a delta in channel power between adjacent channels
or groups of channels. It may be observed multiple times in the spectrum. Adjacency misalignment issues can be
attributable to RF combining or source issues at the headend or hub or in a node utilizing a broadcast/narrowcast
overlay or a split band configuration when the two spectrums are not combined correctly. In some cases, adjacency
misalignment can occur where local channels are inserted in the spectrum, for instance, at a multiple dwelling unit
(MDU).
When adjacency misalignment is observed, the lower power channels may indicate poor performance, seen as a
degraded receive modulation error ratio (RxMER) when the delta between channels is large. This condition can
manifest as lost packets, video tiling, freezing, or, in very extreme cases, black or frozen screens at a customer's
home. Because adjacency misalignment is usually introduced very early in the downstream signal path, it has the
potential to affect a significant number of customers. The mechanics of how an adjacency misalignment can occur
are further described below.
The downstream spectrum received at the CPE is produced by combining multiple non-overlapping regions of
spectrum for different services. In a highly simplified example, shown in Figure 14, a three-input combiner is used
to combine three types of services: video, high-speed data (HSD) plus voice, and video on demand (VOD).


                    Video Services 96 to 504 MHz
                                                                    Combiner




                        HSD/Voice 504 to 552 MHz                                           To Transmitter



                     VOD Services 552 to 870 MHz


                               [The services can be placed anywhere in the allocated spectrum.]

                             Figure 14 - Example of a Simplified Three-Input Combiner


The combiner takes the three service bandwidths and frequency multiplexes the channels from 96 MHz to 870 MHz
for transmission in a single cable, which then feeds an amplifier or an optical transmitter. Adjacent channel
misalignment can be introduced if the channel signal levels are not set correctly before combining or if the combiner
losses are not accounted for properly.
In an ideal scenario, every channel is set at the same power level, resulting in a flat spectrum. However, if the
combined channels arrive at the headend optical transmitter at differing channel power levels, the result will be a
misaligned RF spectrum affecting all customers downstream of that transmitter.
As a point of reference, the Federal Communications Commission [47 C.F.R. §76.605] defines the maximum signal
level of an adjacent analog TV channel as being within 3 dB of any visual carrier within 6 MHz.




09/27/23                                           CableLabs                                                            43
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 44 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                      CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


To maintain good engineering practices, this maximum signal level rule should also apply for adjacent digital
channels. From the headend, the digital channel levels should be within 1 dB of each other if they are using the same
modulation order (e.g., 256-QAM).

Digital Channel Mask
Each digital channel has a spectral mask that defines how much energy is confined within the channel bandwidth
and the allowed noise and distortion that can impact adjacent and non-adjacent channels. Figure 15 shows the
maximum amplitude limits of the noise and distortions that can be found in adjacent and non-adjacent channels
relative to the channel maximum power, referenced as 0 dBc.
                                            OCS S S g e C a     e Spect a   as
                                    dBc
                                     0
                                     -10
                                     -20
                                     -30
                                     -40
                                     -50
                                     -60
                                     -70



                                Figure 15 - DOCSIS Single Channel Spectral Mask


A misaligned channel has an impact on the adjacent and non-adjacent spectrum. As shown in Figure 16, the red
channel is 10 dB above where it should be, as are the channel limits as defined by the channel's spectral mask. The
misalignment has a negative impact on the other channels, which may present at the CPE as degraded RxMER.




                         Figure 16 - Effect of Channel Misalignment on Other Channels


Figure 17 shows an ideal spectral input to the transmitter, flat across the spectrum with each channel utilizing SC-QAM
signals with the same modulation order, typically 256-QAM. Each channel is set at the same power level. In contrast,
Figure 18 shows channels that are not aligned properly prior to insertion into the combiner or transmitter. Once this
alignment has been introduced into the downstream path, it cannot be changed in the field from a channel power
relationship.

                   Figure 18 - Spectral Input with Channels Set with Misaligned Power Levels


Other plant impairments can also cause a change in adjacent power levels. These impairments impact the in-channel
flatness as compared to a channel power level change. Figure 18 shows three adjacencies to measure: second to third
channels, fourth to fifth channels, and fifth to sixth channels.



                                            Lower                        Higher
                                          Frequency                    Frequency




           Figure 19 - Spectral Input Misalignment Caused by Plant Impairments (Not by Power Level)


Figure 19 shows misalignment on non-adjacent channels. Non-adjacent channel misalignment should be analyzed
with the engineering performance parameters outlined in the previous section. A single misaligned channel will have
an impact on the other analog or digital channels in the spectrum. In this instance, there are two adjacencies to
measure: second to third and third to fourth.
Figure 20 shows data for a single CPE. It has a misalignment between adjacent SC-QAM channels of about 8 dB.
Each vertical scale division is 5 dB. Depending on the level relationship to the control carriers, the channels at the
higher power level may have a significant impact on downstream performance.




09/27/23                                          CableLabs                                                             45
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 46 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                      CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


                                    Measure the delta between the
                                         adjacent channels




                        Figure 20 - Adjacent Misalignment in SC-QAM Channels in a CPE


The level difference of the misalignment is determined by first measuring the power levels of the lower CTA-L#
channel number and the higher CTA-H# channel number. The delta is then recorded by subtracting the CTA-L#
power level from the CTA-H# power level.
In Figure 21, data extracted from PNM show multiple CPEs connected to the same node and/or transmitter. The
channel alignment issue will be found at either the headend or hub and is readily visible at the node and points
downstream from the node. As shown in the figure, the lower channels may be part of a broadcast or narrowcast
overlay that is not set correctly in the headend or hub.




            Figure 21 - Adjacent Misalignment in Multiple CPEs with the Same Node and/or Transmitter


The goal of this measurement is to identify adjacency misalignment issues within an operator's network. As evidenced
by customer contacts into operators' care and support centers, it is assumed that the impact to the customer experience
will become more noticeable as adjacency misalignment increases in amplitude. When adjacency misalignments are
corrected, the customer experience should improve and be quantifiable by a decrease in customer contacts.
By implementing the logic to comprehensively measure adjacency misalignments, operators can measure the prevalence
of the issue and its true impact on operations.

5.2.6.3.1       Adjacency and Channel Alignment Thresholds
The default adjacency detection logic in spectral impairment detector is contained in the AdjacentIdentifier code
within the ident package of the software library. The following values are used within the analyzer and may be
adjusted to accommodate differences in RF environments or operational standards.
    •    Minimum dB delta – 2.9 – the absolute minimum delta in dB units between two channels that would qualify
         as adjacency.
    •    Maximum dB delta – 14 – the absolute maximum delta in dB units between two channels that would qualify
         as adjacency. Set this to avoid false positive in adjacent channels.




09/27/23                                              CableLabs                                                    46
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 47 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                         CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


    •     Maximum ICFR – 4 – the absolute maximum delta in dB units between two channels that would qualify as
          adjacency. In-channel frequency response is the difference between the highest value and lowest value in
          the channel. Set this to avoid false positive in adjacent channels.
    •     Maximum tilt – 1.75 – the largest absolute slope that a channel can have before the data are not used for
          adjacency detection. Set this to avoid false positive in adjacent channels. Slope or tilt is measured by linear
          regression.
    •     High tilt individual channel – 2.5 – the maximum slope in a series of individual channels following a given
          channel being used to measure for adjacency.
    •     Channel flatness check – 3 – the number of channels used to check for individual channel high tilt.
    •     High tilt cumulative – 4.0 – the maximum absolute tilt across a cumulative series of channels following a
          given channel being used to measure for adjacency.
    •     Minimum frequency – 110000000 – the minimum frequency that can be used to trigger adjacency; this
          should be set to avoid false positives in lower frequencies.
    •     Maximum frequency – 700000000 – the maximum frequency that can be used to trigger adjacency; this
          should be set to avoid false positives in higher frequencies.
    •     High channel tilt count – 10 – the maximum number of channels in the whole spectrum that can have high
          tilt before the data sample must be discarded and not used for adjacency detection.
    •     High channel delta count – 9 – the maximum number of channels that can have a significant delta detected
          between adjacent digital channels before the data sample must be discarded and not used for adjacency
          detection. A lot of variability suggests something other than adjacency.

5.2.6.4    Filters and Missing Channels
Improper filtering creates an RF impediment that may affect multiple channels at a time. Filters have been and
continue to be used to remove unwanted signals in the RF path based on the customer's needs. When viewed at the
CPE or other test equipment, the presence of a filter is characterized by the lack of channels in a specified
bandwidth. Four types of filters can be found in the field. Examples of the four filters' frequency response
characteristics are shown in Figure 22.
    (a) Band-pass filter—passes frequencies within a specified range and rejects frequencies outside that range
    (b) Band-stop filter—rejects frequencies within a specified range and passes frequencies outside that range
    (c) Low-pass filter—passes frequencies below a specified frequency and rejects frequencies above the
        specified frequency
    (d) High-pass filter—passes frequencies above a specified frequency and rejects frequencies below the
        specified frequency

                                         Frequency                                  Frequency



                   (a)                                            (c)
                                                                        Amplitude
                         Amplitude




                   (b)                                            (d)
                         Amplitude




                                                                        Amplitude




        Figure 22 - Filter Response Types: (a) Band-Pass Filter, (b) Band-Stop Filter, (c) Low-Pass Filter,
                                               (d) High-Pass Filter

PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                          CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


Figure 23 shows example FBC data for two kinds of filters.

                                                  Band-Stop Filter




                                                  High-Pass Filter




                        Figure 23 - Example FBC Data for Band-Stop and High-Pass Filters

An alternate way of describing filter types is to categorize them as video filters, data-only filters, noise filters, and
Multimedia over Coax Alliance (MoCA®) filters.
    •     Video filters are band-stop or notch filters that block a specific frequency range, and they were used to
          differentiate service types in analog plant; these should be found and removed to provide full spectrum
          access for the customer. They can usually be found at the tap.
    •     Data-only filters are band-stop or notch filters that block video services and pass DOCSIS channels.
    •     Noise filters are usually high-pass filters that block all or a portion of the upstream spectrum to prevent
          upstream noise coming from the home or drop from entering the distribution plant. These can also usually
          be found at the side of the home or at the tap. Though these do prevent upstream noise from entering the
          distribution plant, the ideal solution is to find the ingress source and remove the cause, which is likely a
          loose connector or shield issue. Sources of ingress can also be sources of egress or RF leakage.
    •     MoCA is an in-home networking technology. MoCA point of entry filters installed at the demarcation point
          (e.g., ground block) can help improve MoCA performance within the premises and prevent the MoCA
          signal from entering the distribution network and interfering with MoCA devices in nearby homes.

5.2.6.4.1      Filters and Missing Channels Thresholds
The default adjacency detection logic in spectral impairment detector is contained in the MissingChannelsIdentifier
code within the ident package of the software library. The following values are used within the analyzer and may be
adjusted to accommodate differences in RF environments or operational standards.
    •     Minimum digital channels – 68 – the minimum number of active channels needed to indicate a valid
          channel; anything lower is a missing channel impairment.
    •     ICFR threshold – 18.0 – any channel power over this threshold is marked as an active channel.
    •     Minimum dB threshold – -35 – devices with uniformly low power are discarded and may indicate a filter.
    •     Old plant maximum frequency – 575000000 – a way of identifying what is commonly referred to as 550 MHz
          plant that is not using higher spectrum ranges and can be ignored for this test.

5.2.6.5     Suckout
A suckout (a type of notch) is an RF impairment that often spans multiple CTA channels. It is characterized by a
concave notch with sinusoidal boundaries with attenuation in amplitude/power in the frequency domain, often
observed over several megahertz. Typically, suckouts are caused by mechanical or grounding issues in active or
passive network elements such as seizures, connectors, lids, or fittings. They can be attributable to multiple
mismatches that are evenly spaced through the network. Each mismatch adds to the width and depth of the notch at
the frequency of the suckout. An example is the repetitive impedance discontinuities created by the so-called "mold
spike" in some disc-style dielectric cables. In simple terms, a suckout is the result of an impedance mismatch.
Structurally, the cause of the suckout resembles an RLC circuit that is tuned to a specific frequency for the purpose
of filtering an unwanted signal from the spectrum.


09/27/23                                         CableLabs                                                                 48
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 49 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                      CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


The effect that a suckout has on the customer's experience depends on the depth and width of the notch. The notch
can happen anywhere along the RF distribution path, making it one of the more complex issues to troubleshoot. The
FBC tool can assist in determining where in the network the issue may originate and if the issue is periodic. Examining
the spectrum from several CPEs associated with the node can determine if the issue is affecting multiple CPEs or a
single CPE. This approach, coupled with knowledge of failure modes, can help operators localize the failure.
Anecdotally, operators have seen the suckout issue as one of the most prevalent RF impairments affecting spectral
performance; however, no quantification has been made to date on the prevalence and scale of the impairments.
From a customer point of view, a suckout may or may not have a significant impact on their service experience
depending on the services to which they are subscribed and the location (and severity) of the suckout in the spectrum.
Figure 24, Figure 25, and Figure 26 show various examples of suckouts. Each of these types of suckout will be seen
in the network, but the slopes may not be as straight and the curves not as sharp as they appear in these diagrams. In
the figures, each vertical line represents a 6-MHz bandwidth, and each horizontal line represents 5 dB.




                                        Figure 24 - Typical Spectrum Notch




Figure 25 - Wide Spectrum Notch, Typical Depth                  Figure 26 - Shallow Spectrum Notch, Typical Width


Figure 27 and Figure 28 provide additional examples of suckouts demonstrated by PNM. The slopes are more jagged,
but especially note the difference in the depth of each notch and the width of the affected spectrum.




                                  Figure 27 - Suckouts as Demonstrated by PNM



09/27/23                                       CableLabs                                                           49
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 50 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                        CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927




             Figure 28 - Expanded View of Suckout Notch and Impact on Several Digital Channels


A suckout can be caused by several mechanisms, some of which are described below.
    •    Craft-related issues are a significant cause of suckouts and usually occur because of loose seizure or assembly
         or retainer screws, incorrect hardline connector pin length, improper torque on housing lids, or kinks in
         cable.

    •    Manufacturing problems can also lead to suckouts in spectrum. Causes include cold solder joints, equipment
         not meeting specifications, and casting defects.
    •    Grounding problems on amplifier modules are known to cause suckouts.
    •    Design limitations can also lead to suckout problems. Some hardline disk dielectric coaxial cables can have
         suckouts in the frequency domain, typically occurring at about 550 MHz or 1.1 GHz, depending on the
         cable's vintage. This type of hardline cable is no longer manufactured but may still be found in some cable
         networks.
See Appendix I for a more complete treatment of this topic as a general spectrum fault.

5.2.6.5.1      Suckout Thresholds
The default adjacency detection logic in spectral impairment detector is contained in the SuckoutIdentifier code
within the ident package of the software library. Several parameters are used to limit false positive and false negative
detection of a suckout, and those are described below. However, note that those parameters alone would not
differentiate a suckout from an amplitude ripple, for example; therefore, they are only a start and would need to be
adjusted for specific channel plans and situations.
    •    Minimum dB delta used for suckout detection, likely a different value than for wave (amplitude ripple)
         detection
    •    Minimum and maximum frequencies for the search interval
    •    Individual channel tilt (slope) and cumulative channel tilt
    •    Maximum dB drop or delta between adjacent channels



09/27/23                                         CableLabs                                                            50
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 51 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                         CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


    •     Number of adjacent channels to check when looking for high tilt
    •     Window of channels over which to average dB values for the delta calculations
    •     Number of channels over which a decline continues to help differentiate a suckout from an adjacency
    •     Maximum channel gap allowed so that a suckout is not detected over large vacant spectrum gaps
    •     The sum of the individual channel tilt and channel ICFR to filter out non-digital channels

5.2.6.6    Ingress
Over-the-air ingress is an RF impairment that occurs when signals external to the cable network find a means of
getting into the plant and propagating with the desired signals. The cable network's signals operate on frequencies
that are often used for other purposes in the over-the-air environment. This "sharing" of frequencies is known as
frequency reuse, and as long as the shielding integrity of the cable network is intact, cable signals and over-the-air
signals on the same frequencies can coexist. Ingress is an unwanted interaction between electronic systems (over-
the-air and enclosed). The coaxial network is designed and built to minimize the effects of the over-the-air signals
on the cable network's signals (and vice-versa).
Over-the-air ingress has several causes, all related to degraded shielding integrity. Typically, it is located at the
customer premise, either at the drop or, more likely, in the home. Examples of causes include inadequate shielding
at the receiver, damaged cable, node or amplifier RF shielding issues, or poor coax connections, and they can come
from a number of sources. Ingress often affects relatively consistent bands of spectrum, most of which are allocated
and licensed by the FCC or other governmental agency. The most common types of over-the-air ingress are described
below.
    •     FM Ingress—FM ingress is the result of FM radio signals entering the plant from FM radio broadcasting
          sources. It is easily recognizable by the pattern it creates. It affects downstream frequencies between
          approximately 88 MHz and 108 MHz. The FCC allocates a 200-kHz per-channel bandwidth within the
          88-MHz to 108-MHz range for this service, and FM ingress often exhibits spikes in spectral power at odd
          intervals of tenths of a megahertz (e.g., 90.1, 90.3, 90.5 MHz) because the center frequency is located at
          half the channel bandwidth of the FM channel. Frequencies of concern differ throughout the world
          according to the FM broadcast frequencies used.
    •     Cellular Ingress—Cellular ingress is the result of cellular devices and base station transmissions leaking
          into the cable plant. Most often, cellular interference exists at 700 MHz and above, though in some
          countries, the 600-MHz band is now used for over-the-air telecommunications services.
    •     VHF Ingress—VHF ingress covers any ingress not classified as FM ingress in the range 108 MHz to
          300 MHz. 7 Interference in these bands can arise from numerous sources, including broadcast television,
          radio location, amateur radio, land mobile, marine radio, aeronautical navigation and communications, and
          military communications.
    •     UHF Ingress—UHF ingress covers any ingress not classified as cellular ingress in the range of 300 MHz
          to 3 GHz. Interference in these bands can arise from numerous sources, including broadcast television,
          amateur radio, land mobile, mobile satellite, aircraft navigation and communications, and military
          communications.
    •     Direct Pickup—Direct pickup occurs when the interference enters a set-top box, cable modem, television
          set, or other device directly without the need for loose or poorly shielded cables. Poor shielding in the
          device's case can allow direct pickup of outside RF signals by the circuits in the equipment.
As shown above, there are numerous potential sources of ingress into a cable system, and, accordingly, their spectral
profiles can be quite complicated to characterize, identify, and fix. Ingress introduced early in the downstream path
has a much higher probability of a having negative customer impact depending on the frequency, bandwidth, and
amplitude of the ingress signal. If the plant fault is significant, the ingress can severely impair the downstream signal
to the CPE.


7 By definition, the very high frequency, or VHF, spectrum is from 30 MHz to 300 MHz.




09/27/23                                         CableLabs                                                           51
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 52 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                      CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


Ingress can also sit close to the noise floor under the CTA channels. Such cases may be seen in the guard bands
between channels as an observable increase in the noise floor or a sharp spike in the captured spectrum. Measuring
wide-band ingress under the CTA channels is difficult and highly dependent on the resolution at which the spectral
response is captured. More study is required to determine if the 30-kHz resolution bandwidth will make capturing
ingress in the guard band feasible.
In some systems, the power of the ingress signals creates interference persistent enough to leave certain CTA
channels impaired or unusable. Based on historical data, no signal is transported by the operator in these channels,
and they are labeled as "poison channels." When a local solution is found and these channels demonstrate a clear
spectral response, these channels can be loaded, resulting in the addition of useable spectrum for additional services.
Ingress can be sporadic in nature and is frequency independent. An ingress signal at 100 MHz does not mean that a
700-MHz signal will be observed; the opposite is also true. If the conditions in the plant allow an ingress signal at
UHF channel 21 (512 MHz to 518 MHz), then the spectrum around 100 MHz and 700 MHz may not show ingress
as an issue. Alternatively, as can be seen in the examples of ingress in Figure 29, ingress may be seen throughout the
spectrum. The second illustration in the figure shows the potential of using the guard bands to evaluate potential
ingress issues. Figure 30 shows a significant ingress event with multiple issues, the ingress in the FM band being the
most evident. It also shows periodicity issues that may clear up when the source of the ingress is found.




                                          Figure 29 - Examples of Ingress

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 53 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                      CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927




                Figure 30 - Example of a Significant Ingress Event with Several Types of Ingress


5.2.6.6.1      Additional Noise Types
There are other types of noise causes to consider, including switching power supply noise, fluorescent light ballast
noise, and power line gap noise. When switching power supply noise or fluorescent light ballast noise enters the
plant, it may appear in the upstream frequencies and likely near a gap in the network that coincides with a power
supply or ground fault near it. Power line gap noise is created when sparking occurs between two conductive objects
on a utility pole within the electromagnetic field produced by the current in the power line, potentially leading to
burst noise, often in upstream frequencies. As such, it will appear where coax networks are near power equipment.
These types of noise can be detected by some cable modems that can capture spectrum in these upstream
frequencies.
Other possible sources of burst noise include automobile ignitions, static from lightning, and arc welders.

5.2.6.6.2      Noise Thresholds
The default adjacency detection logic in spectral impairment detector is contained in the NoiseIdentifier code within
the ident package of the software library. One value is used within the analyzer and may be adjusted to accommodate
differences in RF environments or operational standards.
    •     Analog dB threshold = -20

5.2.6.7     Passive Intermodulation
Passive intermodulation (PIM)—Intermodulation distortion products generated when two or more signals with
different frequencies pass through a passive device or component that has nonlinear properties. The nonlinear
properties of a device or component can behave somewhat like a mixer circuit, creating distortion products
mathematically related to the original signals (see Figure 31).

The following examples can be sources of PIM.
            •   Surface oxidation or corrosion on metallic conductors in the RF path
            •   Loose metal-to-metal contacts
            •   Ferromagnetic materials in the signal path, such as ferrites in splitters
            •   Physical contact between dissimilar metals
            •   Insufficiently thick plating on metal components, such as connectors
            •   Improperly tightened connectors, center conductor seizure mechanisms, etc.

5.2.6.8          Resonant Peaking
Resonant peaking is an RF spectrum impairment that can affect a series of RF or CTA channels. It is easily
recognized when viewing the full spectrum and observing the frequency response. Resonant peaking is observed as
an upward change in amplitude from a linear or flat spectral frequency response that peaks and then reduces in
amplitude.
More specifically, resonant peaking is characterized as an upward perturbation of the spectrum with convex and
sinusoidal boundaries. It can be caused by one or more faulty network elements along the delivery path. Typically,
the issue resides in an active device such as a node or an amplifier. If multiple amplifiers have a spectrum signature
with a peak at the same frequency, they become additive, causing the peak to increase in amplitude and the associated
slopes to expand in bandwidth. A faulty passive element in the plant can exhibit what appears to be a peak.
It is important to note that resonant effects can be sporadic in nature—they may be visible for a while at a particular
frequency but later disappear and reappear; also, the peak(s) can vary in frequency over time. As such, they can be
complicated to diagnose. The peaking can also be temperature dependent in that temperature variability can change
the effectiveness of the grounding or shielding or the quality of the connection.
The location in the delivery path where the resonant peak is introduced will determine the overall impact. The peak's
relationship to the control carriers can impact the performance of the other channels being distributed.
            •   If the peak happens at the amplifier automatic gain control carrier, the amplifier or series of amplifiers will
                react on a decibel-for-decibel basis to impact adjacent channels or the full spectrum.



09/27/23                                                             CableLabs                                                                     54
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 55 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                                       CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


    •    If the peak happens away from the automatic gain control carrier, the impact will be observed in the CSO,
         CTB, and RxMER performance.
This impairment can manifest at the CPE as degraded RxMER, poor codeword performance, or packet loss, which
could result in slow data transfer speeds, tiling, or freezing, depending on which channels are affected. The following
figures show examples of the effects of resonant peaking. In Figure 32, it is seen at 716 MHz and has an impact on
four channels on either side of the resonant peak.

In Figure 33, observe the channels impacted by the resonant peak. If the change in amplitude across the channel is
greater than 3 dB, the channel may start to show impairments. In this example, the change from normal to the peak
is approximately 17 dB, resulting in a performance impact that may be seen across the spectrum.

30 kHz, giving the peak's spectrum a flat-top appearance, and the delta between the top trace and the lower traces is
approximately 18 dB. The traces show that a common device is causing the resonant peaking. Additionally, as shown
in Figure 35, this spectrum has additional alignment issues beyond the resonant peaking that need to be corrected.

One common cause of resonant peaking is poor or degraded grounding in an active device on the plant, which can
result from loose screws or bolts, loose covers, poorly grounded circuit boards, or loose or corroded connectors.

5.2.6.9    Improper Amplifier Alignment
Improper amplifier alignment can result in some interesting spectrum captures that look like boosted signals in
certain frequencies. As such, they can resemble, and certainly relate to, resonant peaking but actually are from a
different cause.
Improper amplifier alignment and padding is discussed in Section 7.6 of this document, as, in some cases, it can
appear as an uneven noise floor.

5.2.6.10 Roll-off
Spectral roll-off is characterized by a gradual, non-linear decrease in amplitude and power over the frequency band, in
frequency order. It is most often found toward the high end of the downstream frequency range; e.g., closer to 1 GHz.
Roll-off in the spectrum can be caused by a number of issues in the network.
    •     Filters can be detected with a roll-off in a frequency edge, as discussed previously. Filters may be near
          homes or at taps but may also be placed near actives.
    •     Some network components can produce a roll-off because of their design characteristics; for example, a
          750-MHz component in a 1-GHz network, tolerance problems, or improper installation or alignment.
    •     When not caused by an intended filter or incorrect network component, roll-off in a spectrum is most
          commonly caused by water-soaked passive network elements (caused by water migration through the
          cable) and can be found with water-damaged cables or devices.
    •     There are also cases where a suckout or very short distance amplitude ripple appears like a roll-off, though
          these are different impairment types that can look similar.

5.2.6.10.1     Discussion
    1.    Devices in the network have a pass band through which frequencies will pass with a minimal loss of signal
          strength. Typical pass bands used to design components in the past include 450, 550, 650, 750, and 870 MHz.
          In some cases, cable networks transmit signals at frequencies slightly above these pass bands. If components
          designed for these pass bands are in the network (for example, older 550-MHz passives in a 750-MHz
          network), they may be identified by roll-off of the spectrum above their designed pass bands as signals are
          added. Components with the same pass band characteristics will be shown as additive and will produce a
          sharper roll-off. In these cases, the signal strength at higher frequencies degrades at a much more rapid pace
          than lower frequencies, resulting in spectral roll-off than can affect the customer experience depending on
          how far the customer's device is from the point of transmission.
    2.    Individual elements along the transmission path, such as amplifiers and, less commonly, defective cable,
          can produce roll-off characteristics, especially when a signal must propagate through numerous amplifiers
          that are not configured correctly for the network. For example, equalizers in these nodes or amplifiers may
          have an upper limit of 750 MHz even though the plant is trying to pass 870 MHz.



09/27/23                                        CableLabs                                                            56
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 57 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                                CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


     3.   Network designs that have an equalizer placed mid-span between the amplifier and the CPE can have the
          same roll-off issue if they were designed for a lower frequency than what is trying to be passed.
     4.   Some networks may use older coaxial cable that was not designed for the extended frequencies in use
          today. For example, the coax may have typical losses up to 650 MHz, but the greater losses would be
          anticipated at higher frequencies. There are other potential causes of additional loss in a coaxial cable, such
          as water migration damage caused by improper installation or a plant fault at a connector. Loss may appear
          either as roll-off or excess loss depending on where it occurs in the network.
     5.   In more limited and extreme cases, what appears to be roll-off may be something else. For example, a large
          suckout at the high end of the spectrum with only one side of the suckout visible might appear to be a roll-
          off upon a quick visual inspection. Similarly, a standing wave can sometimes be misidentified as a suckout
          or a roll-off.
Spectral roll-off may cause tiling or freezing of video channels. Depending on the slope of each individual channel
in the roll-off and the power level at the receiver, the quality of service may be very poor or even nonexistent.
Anecdotally, field technicians have observed roll-off as one of the more prevalent RF impediments on the plant.
However, to date, no actual quantification of the prevalence of this impairment has been made. To that point, as the
roll-off decreases the channel power of each channel in the roll-off region, the likelihood of a poor customer
experience, and eventually a corresponding negative customer service touch point, are correlated. This outcome,
however, needs to be tested and confirmed with data.
In both examples shown in Figure 36, the roll-off appears to start at approximately 750 MHz. The end of the roll-off
is not shown because of the lack of additional carriers above 780 MHz. The slope per channel above 760 MHz may
be impacting the performance of those channels.




                      Figure 36 - Examples of Spectral Roll-off at the High End of the Spectrum


5.2.6.11 Amplitude Ripple
Amplitude ripple (aka standing waves 8) indicates RF impairments that can affect the entire spectrum. Plant
impairments are most often caused by impedance mismatches that occur somewhere between the node and the
modem. In the downstream, when seen by a cable modem, energy reflects off of the second impairment, back to the

8 Standing waves are a distribution of fields in a transmission line such as coaxial cable, caused by the interaction of incident and

reflected waves. The presence of standing waves in the transmission line causes amplitude ripple in the frequency domain.


09/27/23                                             CableLabs                                                                   57
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 58 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                     CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


first, and then some of that energy continues downstream, but delayed, causing an impact to the signal that appears
as a standing wave in the spectrum data. When observed in an amplitude-vs.-frequency display, the standing wave
appears as a periodic amplitude change or a sinusoidal wave-like appearance in the frequency domain.
The heights of the standing wave peaks will vary. The greater the delta in megahertz between the peaks, the shorter
the distance to the anomaly (or, more accurately, the shorter the length of an echo tunnel). The user can examine the
PNM spectrum display to determine the distance between mismatches; the following formulas can be used to
calculate the distance between impedance mismatches.
                                             Distance (feet) = 492 × Vp/f
                                           Distance (meters) = 150 × Vp/f
where
    Vp = velocity of propagation in decimal form (velocity factor) in the network cable, typically 87% (0.87), and
    f    = frequency of the periodicity, standing wave frequency expressed in megahertz, estimated as the
           frequency difference between adjacent peaks or troughs.
A mismatch can be caused by any active or passive network element (e.g., amplifier, splitter) that connects to the
coaxial cable that eventually leads into the customer's home. Operators can utilize data extracts, such as those shown
in Figure 37, to measure the exact prevalence and impact of this RF impairment. Using the data from multiple CPEs
associated with a node can help determine whether the standing wave event is isolated or affecting several
customers.




                                     Figure 37 - Example of Amplitude Ripple

At times, amplitude ripple can appear to be non-uniform in magnitude because of transmission line properties.
Though this is not always the case, it is important to note that this non-uniform amplitude ripple can often be
observed.
When amplitude ripples are not uniform, they are often considered to be compound amplitude ripples because there
are more than two significant impedance mismatches leading to multiple and complex RF echoes that appear in
spectrum capture. Note that amplifiers and other devices with a poor return loss provide a reflection point [Hranac
Return Loss].

5.2.6.12 Water Waves
Cables in cable systems commonly become water soaked. Regardless of whether they are situated underground or
overhead, cables can become damaged or otherwise deteriorate, allowing water to ingress and eventually migrate
through the length of a cable.
It is fair to say that operators have already had to deal with the outcome of water-soaked cables for a long time.
Unfortunately, because of the lack of detection and classification of water damage, the repairs can be inconsistent
and oftentimes unpredictable. When the technician is able to acquire some information about the likely cause of the
problem, knowing what to look for is easier, thus, troubleshooting can be done faster, and repairs are more likely to
be effective.


09/27/23                                       CableLabs                                                          58
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 59 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                     CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


5.2.6.12.1    Customer Impact
The impact on the customer's experience can vary depending on the cable's exposure to the elements. In this
subsection, the effect on customer service from water intrusion in cables is discussed. See Section 9 for more
information on deciding whether to address a found water issue based on severity.

5.2.6.12.1.1 Customer Experience: A Typical Example
When water damage occurs, it is common to hear from customers that service has occasionally been poor and
unreliable. In Figure 38, full-band capture of spectrum from a cable modem was used to identify an individual
subscriber drop that had water damage, specifically by the unique signature in the displayed frequency response.
As Figure 38 shows, the response has a non-periodic wave shape, and attenuation increases dramatically at higher
frequencies. Remote polling of the modem showed that most of the downstream SC-QAM signals had poor
performance, as shown in Figure 39. The upstream was relatively unaffected.




                    Figure 38 - Water-Damaged Cable Frequency Response Prior to Repair


Note the non-periodic wave shape in the FBC response in Figure 38, as well as the higher attenuation at higher
frequencies. This example occurred when abrasion damaged the cable's jacket, allowing water to enter the cable.
As seen in Figure 39, most of the downstream SC-QAM signals have low signal level and degraded RxMER,
indicated in red shaded boxes. The upstream was relatively unaffected.




               Figure 39 - Impaired Downstream SC-QAM Power and RxMER Levels per Channel


Technicians are able to find and fix this type of problem without having to enter the premises. Figure 40 shows the
coax jacket, which had been damaged by abrasion from the electrical service drop. The damaged coax jacket
allowed water to enter the cable and travel inside the cable all the way to the ground block. Figure 41 shows water
coming out of the connector at the ground block end of the drop.

                Figure 41 - Water Coming Out of the End of the Connector at the Ground Block

FBC screen shot after the new drop was installed, and Figure 43 shows the post-repair SC-QAM performance.
