7.4    Cyclic Prefix, Roll-off, and Amplitude Ripple
Cyclic prefix (CP) is a copy of the end of a symbol that is added to the beginning of the same symbol to help
mitigate the effects of micro-reflections and similar impairments. CP allows recovery of the signal in the presence of
RF echoes, which are caused by impedance mismatches in the system.
CP duplicates portions of the symbol in the time domain, thereby reducing spectral efficiency. By increasing CP, the
symbol is longer in duration; thus, the symbol rate is reduced. The CP duration also must be longer than the roll-off
period (RP). Longer RPs provide smaller taper regions (TRs). TRs cause portions of the downstream spectrum
around the signal to be unusable. A larger TR often results in a reduction of the modulated spectrum of the channel,
which is another factor that contributes to reduction of efficiency.
TR and RP are coupled, meaning the choice of one constrains the other. Energy between an SC-QAM and an
OFDM/A channel can interfere, so some care must be taken with the selection of TR and RP. Table 7 provides
guidelines for trading off the TP and RP (for the upstream). Likewise, Table 55 from [PHYv3.1] provides guidelines
for 2K and 4K FFT (for the downstream). Because these are simply guidelines, operators can choose to follow them,
or they can decide on their own. Operators are free to choose the use of GBs as they like or even allow for the
minimal GBs possible. In the downstream, an operator can choose to place an SC-QAM close to the OFDM channel
without overlap and reduce the degradation to the OFDM channel by lowering the bit loading of the subcarriers at
the band edge adjacent to the SC-QAM channel. In the upstream, an operator can place an SC-QAM as close to the
OFDMA channel as possible, potentially with overlap, and let the CMTS manage the grants given so as to minimize
interference. In any case, however, a GB (downstream) or channel spacing (upstream) would be set, and that may be
based on recommendations of the TR and RP.



09/27/23                                       CableLabs                                                         161
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 162 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                        CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


See [SCTE 270], the "Mathematics of Cable" operational practice, for more details. This document shows how to
calculate spectral efficiency, bits per second per hertz, for OFDM channels.

7.4.1    A Reasonable Procedure for Settings
Cable operators can choose TR, RP, and CP as they desire for the channel setup they like. Regardless of the GB set,
the CP has to account for the TR, and the rest of it can compensate for echoes in the signal. [CM-TR-Efficiency]
explains much of these tradeoffs and provides guidance for upstream and downstream settings.
For a section of upstream frequency, which covers an OFDMA carrier and one or two SC-QAM neighbors, because
the un-energized subcarriers of an OFDMA channel are not included in the occupied bandwidth of the OFDMA
channel, the frequency bandwidth (BW) covered by the OFDMA and SC-QAM carriers is BWofdma + (BWsc +
GB)*n where n = 1 or 2 depending on whether one or both band edges of the OFDMA carrier have adjacent
SC-QAM neighbors.
For the downstream, the OFDM bandwidth consists of modulated and unmodulated (silent) subcarriers, so that the
frequency covered by the OFDM and SC-QAM carriers would be MBWofdm + SBWofdm + n*BWsc where n = 1 or 2
depending on whether one or both band edges of the OFDM carrier have adjacent SC-QAM neighbors. MBWofdm is
the bandwidth of the modulated subcarriers, and SBWofdm is the bandwidth of the silent subcarriers. Note that in
these examples only the OFDM/A and neighbor SC-QAM channels are accounted for; this can be generalized to
cover more than this subset of channels.
In any case, for upstream and downstream, there is a need to consider the RP when setting the CP. Though operators
can set these parameters to their liking, the resulting RP will reduce the amount of echo that the CP can cover.
Therefore, the following consideration is helpful—let CPecho be the amount of CP needed to correct for a given
specified amount of echo, then set 𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶 ≥ 𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅 + 𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒ℎ𝑜𝑜 .
Note that this equation can be applied in units of microseconds or samples using Tables 9 and 10 from [PHYv3.1] to
translate. Using microseconds as the units helps with translating the echo that is to be covered in terms of the
velocity factor as CPecho = 2*Dft / (VF*C) with the answer in time units, with VF in appropriate units. Note that
VF*C is often expressed in feet per nanosecond in the equation, so for microseconds, remember to take the result
                                                                                                           1
and divide by 1000 to get CP in microseconds for use in the table. For hardline cable with VF = 0.87,           ≅
                                                                                                        𝑉𝑉𝑉𝑉∗𝐶𝐶
1.17 𝑛𝑛𝑛𝑛𝑛𝑛𝑛𝑛𝑛𝑛𝑛𝑛𝑛𝑛𝑛𝑛𝑛𝑛𝑛𝑛𝑛𝑛 𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝 𝑓𝑓𝑓𝑓𝑓𝑓𝑓𝑓. For example, for a CP = 2.5 microseconds, Dft = 1068 feet.
A reasonable process to follow therefore is to choose the FFT size, then choose the channel placement and RP as
desired, then select the CP to cover for the RP and needed echo in the RF signal.

7.4.2    A Note on Spectral Efficiency
To determine the desired CP to use, calculate the efficiency of the selected CP or, better yet, calculate the efficiency
of all combinations of CP and RP. Calculation for spectral efficiency in bits per second per hertz can be found in the
"Mathematics of Cable" operational practice [SCTE 270]. In terms of spectrum, one factor of efficiency can be
thought of as (BWofdma + n*BWsc )/(BWofdma + (BWsc + GB)*n), which accounts for efficiency loss from
un-energized spectrum in the guard bands. However, considering the time domain, more resiliency from the GB
(generally GB is related to TR) traded off with RP, and more resiliency from CP, the optimization becomes more
interesting. Each transmission then has a CP, which includes the RP, so another efficiency loss factor of
FFTd/(FFTd+CP) occurs because of the CP, where FFTd indicates the OFDMA FFT duration. Note that the product
of these two efficiency factors, the guard band efficiency and the cyclic prefix efficiency, contributes to overall
spectral efficiency and system efficiency; this product comprises an upper bound on the system efficiency, which is
a measure of how efficiently the modulation schemes utilize the dedicated spectrum. Note that bit loading can be
reduced (such as in OFDMA subcarriers closer to adjacent SC-QAM channels), which will further reduce system
efficiency. However, this reduction in system efficiency may be countered by reducing the guard band, so there is a
tradeoff in system efficiency, with the guard band efficiency increasing while the bit loading of some subcarriers
reduces the bits per second. These factors need to be considered together.
With equal bit loading across the frequencies and cases, multiply the cyclic prefix efficiency equation and guard
band efficiency equation to get an overall efficiency to facilitate comparisons of different parameter selections. If bit
loading is adjusted in one or more scenarios, then calculate the overall bits that the dedicated spectrum can support
and multiply that by the product of the guard band and cyclic prefix efficiencies.



09/27/23                                        CableLabs                                                           162
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 163 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                        CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


7.4.3    Resiliency Management
For a given selected configuration, the limits to manage include the upstream utilization of the dedicated spectrum
and the echo that can be canceled.
A tradeoff between dedicated spectrum and the presence and time delay of echoes can then be considered.
    •    Probability of grants that fully exhaust the upstream spectrum (100% grant)
    •    Probability of an echo in the system that would impact service
Generally, as operators tend to manage their access networks, it is more likely that a 100% grant will happen more
frequently than a new echo will appear.
Because there are finite choices, one can calculate the results for all combinations and select the ideal one. It is
possible to then pick a CP that covers less than the full potential set of echo possibilities, trading that risk for
additional dedicated spectrum to lower utilization. It is also possible to pick a CP that covers existing echoes only;
set the CP to the largest value possible and reduce it until a problem is found, then set it one level higher to assure
echoes are canceled, then trigger this process again each time the plant is changed or indicated with a new echo
(suffering until corrected). Likewise, it is possible to select an RP (GB) that is smaller to reduce the bit loading in
certain affected frequencies but provide more spectrum for other channels.
Alternative considerations include the demand for the dedicated spectrum (in terms of proportion of time) and the
probability of an echo appearing that is greater than the CP based on estimates of the echo possibilities in the
network. If the echo is easily fully covered without concerns regarding insufficient dedicated spectrum, then the
operator could set the CP for best reliability and then monitor dedicated spectrum utilization through upstream grant
traffic. However, if the echo cannot be fully covered because of upstream traffic needs, then a model of the
probability of an echo appearing that is larger than can be covered by the selected CP is needed for estimating the
reliability of the service provided. The appearance of an echo that cannot be covered by the CP will affect service as
a profile shift or may even result in bit errors if profiles cannot cover the fault.
This assessment generally leads to three reasonable initial settings to consider, aside from accepting default values.
    •    Maximum resiliency through maximum CP, managing resiliency down as capacity is needed
    •    Maximum capacity with sufficient CP resiliency, managing resiliency up as needed
    •    Standard but sufficient CP settings with monitoring of headroom for capacity against demand burstiness
         (probabilistic), as well as the resiliency provided for CP against echoes, RP against channel interference,
         and bit loading of the frequencies
Note that the adjustment should also be minimized because an adjustment to CP requires service downtime. The last
option of the three listed above, with good initial settings, should be effective in minimizing the CP changes needed
to manage the tradeoff.
Some considerations remain for using PNM to help manage the CP and RP tradeoff.
    •    In any optimization approach, the monitored parameters determine the capacity against demand in pairs
         (bandwidth, echo resiliency, channel interference resiliency, and bit loading).
    •    In some of the optimization approaches, one or more of these limits are at the highest setting; therefore,
         they do not need monitoring of the headroom (they only need monitoring of the demand) because any time
         the setting is insufficient, action is required.
    •    In each case, for each monitored parameter, a risk model or probability of exhaust is useful for
         optimization.
    •    When a capacity limit is exceeded, resiliency is challenged so that another resiliency mechanism must
         assist or service is affected (this is always the case with bandwidth).
    •    If adjustments to the resiliency-capacity tradeoff cannot restore service or if risk of service impact is too
         great (based on a cost-benefit model, service level agreement, service policy, specification limit, or
         statistical process control limit, for example), then some sort of maintenance is required. In the short term,
         PNM is likely the solution, as RP can be extended if there is sufficient CP, and CP can be extended if there
         is sufficient dedicated spectrum. This can be most quickly and/or preferably addressed by (1) removing any



09/27/23                                        CableLabs                                                           163
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 164 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                          CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


         echo impairments if they exist; (2) changing the bit loading on frequencies that overlap guard bands, adding
         dedicated spectrum by adding channels or changing channel plans, or adjusting the spectrum usage
         (including FDX); or (3) implementing engineering node splits or technology upgrades. Note that the
         preference for these actions is up to the decision of the implementer operator.

7.4.4    Cyclic Prefix Headroom
Consider the tradeoff between data capacity and CP that can be managed. For a given setting of capacity, the amount
of CP allowed is important.
In a given situation, the amount of CP needed and the amount that exists may not be equal; therefore, the amount of
extra CP that exists can be calculated. For a given echo distance, the amount of extra CP can be calculated as CPextra
= CP-RP-2*Dft / (VF*C) for Dft having different perspectives; two examples are described below.
    •    If Dft is set to the largest possible echo in the system, then a CPextra that is negative indicates echoes are
         possible that cannot be corrected by the CP; a positive value indicates there is room to reduce CP without
         risk.
    •    If Dft is set to the largest existing echo in the system, then a CPextra that is negative indicates the existing
         echo cannot be corrected by the CP; a positive value indicates there is room to reduce CP without risk.
Consider calculating the amount of CPextra and translating that into the amount of capacity that could potentially be
provided by a reduction in CP. For a given CP setting with a positive CPextra, one can consider reducing CP to a
lower setting that may still have a positive CPextra or is otherwise an acceptable risk, then realizing the CP reduction
frees up bandwidth that can be calculated by translating the CP into time. The change in CP, denoted as ∆CP, in
appropriate units of time or bits can indicate the amount of additional upstream grants that can be offered as a result
or the amount of additional downstream data that can be transmitted.
CPextra or ∆CP, in units of data on a per symbol basis, can be thought of as the additional headroom for capacity that
can be mined from reducing CP. Note, however, there is a risk of an impairment appearing that may require
additional CP to protect against.
Also note that with a model of the distribution of potential echoes that could exist on the network, one can calculate
the largest echo that can be covered as CPecho *(VF*C) / 2 = (CP-RP) *(VF*C) / 2 = Dft and then read that point in
the CDF of potential echoes covered to determine the probability of an echo happening that can or cannot be
covered by the CP. This may be a useful indicator of the risk associated with accepting a lower CP to free up
capacity.

7.4.5    The PNM Use Case for Cyclic Prefix and Roll-off Management
The cause of any issues affecting that headroom must be understood for it to be addressed. If the access network was
properly designed and built, degradation may be the main factor to address; however, changes to the network,
including converting to a gateway architecture at the subscriber premises or migrating to higher band splits, can also
affect modem transmit headroom.
If impairments are present and causing excessive net attenuation between the modem and CMTS, then long-loop
ALC will compensate with higher modem transmit power and thus may exhaust the transmit headroom for some
CMs in the system. PNM is the fix. Poor drop conditions, including water in the cable, or other damage and
degradation can lead to higher CM transmit levels to compensate. Unused splitters and other passives at the
subscriber premises can contribute to the problem; remove them if they are not needed. Improperly installed drop
components, such as backwards splitters, should be corrected. In the distribution network, correct alignment of
amplifiers and nodes is critical to ensuring proper modem transmission and, thus, its headroom as well.
Ultimately, when up against one of these headroom measures of merit, the operator has a choice—reduce CMTS
upstream receive set point, reduce the TCS occupied bandwidth, or fix net attenuation problems.
As with any hard constraint on a measure subject to external factors, variability in this performance measure must
also be considered. Instead of waiting until all headroom is exhausted, recognize that coming closer to a headroom
constraint leads to an increase in the risk of an external factor causing the system to violate the constraint, leading to
one of the noted failure modes. PNM helps the operator avoid such problems.




09/27/23                                         CableLabs                                                                 164
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 165 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                                           CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


7.5     Cable Modem Transmission Headroom
The use cases in this section are outlined more completely in [Hranac 2023]. The PNM measurements used here are
described in Section 6.1.
As cable operators extend the frequency range used to provide service in the upstream, they will need to add
channels, which means cable modem transmit power will be spread wider across frequencies, and transmit power is
a constrained resource. Likewise, impairments affect the network's ability to cleanly transmit signals, requiring more
upstream transmit power from the CM for the CMTS to receive and demodulate the signal. In these cases, a
modem's available transmit power spectral density (PSD) is reduced because it must be spread over a wider RF
bandwidth. Therefore, it is incumbent on operators to manage a cable modem's upstream power, which can be
challenging because of a complicated combination of transmitted RF power, dynamic range window (DRW), long-
loop automatic level control (ALC), pre-equalization settings, and channel bandwidth. If a CM's transmitted power
is insufficient, then forward error correction (FEC) errors can result, and bit loading may need to be reduced. Also,
modems can go into partial service, resulting in those modems being unable to achieve advertised upstream speeds.
As the TCP that is transmitted from a given CM increases, the transmit headroom can decrease because the CMs are
transmitting closer to their maximum capability.
The minimum highest value of the total power output of the cable modem Pmax is 65 dBmV, though higher values
are supported in the DOCSIS specifications.
A CM's upstream transmit headroom is the difference, in decibels, between that modem's maximum transmit power
capability and its actual transmit power. In DOCSIS, the CM transmit headroom has two different important
measures: (1) CM TCP headroom and (2) CM minimum channel (Min Ch) headroom. These two headroom values
comprise two different figures-of-merit to characterize a CM's commanded channel power compared to its transmit
power capability. Additionally, each channel of a CM has its own figure-of-merit in this regard: the CM channel
headroom. Note that, in the presence of tilt across two or more upstream channels, the Min Ch headroom will almost
surely be the constraint at risk of being broken before the TCP headroom. Regardless, both measures are important
to manage.

7.5.1     CM TCP Headroom
The following subsection text is taken from [Hranac 2023].
The CM TCP headroom is the difference between the maximum total composite power capability of the CM, Pmax in
[PHYv3.1], and the total composite power currently commanded (called "reported power" in the DOCSIS
specifications) to the CM's transmit channel set (TCS) by the CMTS. In [PHYv3.1], the transmit power commanded
to the nth channel is the product of the PSD commanded to the nth channel, P1.6r_n, and the occupied bandwidth of the
nth channel divided by 1.6 MHz: 14

7.5.2    CM Minimum Channel Headroom
The following subsection text is taken from [Hranac 2023].
In addition to the CM TCP headroom, there is also the CM Min Ch headroom. The CM Min Ch headroom is
important because it quantifies how much increase can occur in the channel with the largest PSD [PHYv3.1] or
largest power, PWRn [PHYv3.0] (with the assumption of 32-QAM or larger constellation using DOCSIS 3.0
technology).
In the DOCSIS 3.1 specifications, the CM Min Ch headroom is the difference between the maximum PSD for the
CM, which is P1.6hi, and the largest PSD commanded in the TCS (largest value of P1.6r_n, over all N channels):

channel headroom is Pload_n, which also equals Phi_n minus PWRn. For DOCSIS 3.1 modems, the nth channel's CM
channel headroom is P1.6hi minus P1.6r_n.

7.5.3    Factors that Affect These Transmit Headroom Measures of Performance
There are several factors and scenarios leading up to exhausting a CM's transmit headroom.
    •    Total composite RF power is insufficient for the channel set—If the TCP in the TCS exceeds Pmax
         supported by the CM, the modem will not have enough power to maintain such transmission. In this case,
         the CM may be forced to drop one or more of the channels and fall back to partial service so that the TCP is
         within the supported range. The headroom indicates how much reserve is available in the current TCP.
    •    TCS occupied bandwidth—If a CM is not already transmitting at its maximum capability, increasing the
         TCS occupied bandwidth also increases the TCS's TCP, which may be enough to violate one or both
         headroom constraints, leading to the inability of the CM to use the channel and falling back to partial
         service or forcing a lower profile at lower power.



15 P
    max represents the CM's TCP capability in [D3.1PHY] but represents an individual channel power limit in [D3.0PHY]. There
is not a term relating to TCP capability in [D3.0PHY], which is why we show how to calculate it.


09/27/23                                                   CableLabs                                                                        166
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 167 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                                                         CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


      •    CMTS target set point—The target set point is the configured upstream receive signal level at the CMTS or
           CCAP. This parameter was called commanded nominal receive power in [PHYv3.0] and earlier. Operators
           can configure the CMTS target set point to a lower or higher level, which effectively decreases or increases
           the TCP of the CM's TCS, and that in turn increases or decreases the effective CM headroom.
      •    Dynamic range window—DRW does not have a direct effect on CM transmit headroom but limits the
           difference between the least and most loaded channels so that the headroom for the most loaded channel
           stays the same. If the DRW is violated, the fidelity requirements for the TCS may be compromised, and,
           eventually, most loaded channel(s) will be dropped from the TCS, forcing the CM into partial service.
      •    Long-loop ALC [Hranac LLALC]—Any change in the net upstream attenuation between modems and the
           CMTS, for any cause, will result in the CMTS telling the affected modems to change their upstream
           transmit power, which may result in a headroom constraint violation, leading to the failures scenarios
           mentioned above.
      •    Upstream pre-equalization—Pre-equalization is normalized; therefore, it does not directly affect the
           transmission headroom, but the uneven loss in the system does require additional transmit power to
           compensate. The impairments that are compensated for by using pre-equalization will require additional
           transmit power from the CM, thus reducing the transmit headroom and potentially both performance
           measures.
When operators consider adding additional channels, the TCP headroom and Min Ch headroom must be considered.
How much occupied bandwidth can be added while maintaining the existing channel powers can be estimated as
                          𝑁𝑁𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒_𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎𝑎_𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝 = ��10(𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶 𝑀𝑀𝑀𝑀𝑀𝑀 𝐶𝐶ℎ ℎ𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒 𝑑𝑑𝑑𝑑⁄10) � − 1� ∗ 𝑁𝑁𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒
Alternatively, the PSD of the highest loaded channel could be lowered to allow the addition of new occupied
bandwidth. The same principles apply with DOCSIS 3.0.

7.6       Addressing Amplifier Nonlinearities Using Full-Band Capture
As described in [O’Dell 2023], when an amplifier in the system is far outside its ideal operating range, the noise
level in the vacant spectrum will differ by frequency in obvious ways, which can be observed in a full-band capture
downstream from the misaligned amplifier. One can look at the low frequency noise floor (unmodulated band) in the
diplex filter region and compare it to the high-frequency noise floor (frequencies higher than the modulated
spectrum), as shown in Figure 156. If there is a significant difference in the average noise levels in these bands,
there is evidence that an amplifier in the system upstream from the CM is misaligned. The noise floor frequencies
may be in a narrow bandwidth (for example, an unoccupied channel such as around 380 MHz in the figure), which
makes it difficult to see. A quick check will determine the low-frequency noise floor average and standard deviation
and the high-frequency noise floor average and standard deviation. Next, do a statistical test of the difference to
determine whether there is reason to reject the null hypothesis that the noise floors in the two frequency bands are
the same. A future study of spectrum captures from cable modems' near-aligned and misaligned amplifiers will
allow determination of parameters for a powerful statistical test.




          Figure 156 - A Cable Modem Spectrum Capture (FBC) Downstream from a Misaligned Amplifier

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 168 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                       CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


7.7    Machine Learning for PNM
Over the last few years, many have applied machine learning to various cable network issues including profile
management, video quality, and of course, PNM impairment detection and classification.
An example of applying machine learning to PNM can be found in [Zhu et al.], and a machine learning approach for
identifying noise can be found in [Ferreira et al.]. The former describes an algorithm in software form; it is available
to participants in the CableLabs Common Code Community (C3) project. Operators and vendors may make use of
the software in various forms.
More recently, Brady Volpe shared experience with applying machine learning to PNM anomaly detection in
[Volpe]. In this work, Volpe pointed out that machine learning is a powerful tool when processing large amounts of
data, which is the case with anomaly detection.
The anomaly detection problem in PNM can be thought of as having three facets: detection, identification, and
quantification. An anomaly must first be detected in the data, then the type of anomaly must be identified so that the
location in the network or the class of component in the network can be inferred. However, to decide on the follow-
up action to take and the timing of that action, the severity of the impairment in terms of current and potential
impact on service must be estimated, which requires quantification.
Machine learning methods are useful for finding patterns in data, so they are promising for problems like anomaly
detection in PNM data. Those methods can also perform well in identifying the type of anomaly and assisting in
quantification because more severe anomalies will be easier to identify clearly. To further improve the effectiveness
of machine learning methods, it is beneficial to apply complementary methods to the same data, the same methods
to data collected from the same network device over time, the same methods to data collected from neighboring
network devices, or a combination of these.
Some impairments can be translated into measurable features that can be quantified while estimating their severities.
Methods like the CableLabs spectral impairment detector, available to C3 members, perform well and do not use
machine learning. See Section 5.2.6 for more details and information about spectral impairment detection.
Current wisdom in [Volpe] suggests that machine learning methods can be complementary to traditional statistical
and rule-based methods. The combination of these two can perform very well to meet the needs of impairment
detection, identification, and quantification. That paper includes experience and field knowledge to support this
wisdom, and the described approach has been successfully implemented. Other operators and vendors are
researching and developing alternate approaches and are finding improvements as well, so there are many potential
approaches to consider.
CableLabs is currently undergoing an effort to extend spectrum impairment detection software to enable machine
learning to augment the current rules-based solutions. With a new architecture and approach to the problem, the
future solution should improve on the current methods while assuring explainability and not requiring reliance on
training data or a ground truth, thus avoiding three of the most significant limitations of many machine-learning
approaches.




09/27/23                                        CableLabs                                                          168
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 169 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                  CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


8 PNM DATA ISSUES
8.1    Data Collection Methods
In DOCSIS 3.0 systems, pre-equalization coefficient and full-band capture (FBC) data are obtained by using SNMP
to request information from CMs and CMTSs or CCAPs. The requested information is described in [PNMP-3.0],
and the SNMP MIB objects are defined in the [DOCS-IF3-MIB].
DOCSIS 3.1 technology introduced a new set of requirements on the CM and CMTS or CCAP related to the
collection and reporting of PNM data (see [PHYv3.1], Section 9, "Proactive Network Maintenance"). DOCSIS 3.1
technology also introduced a new Bulk-Data Transfer mechanism that uses TFTP to upload PNM data to a
destination TFTP server. Configuration of the PNM test execution, file storage, and TFTP destination uses SNMP as
defined in [CM-OSSIv3.1] and [CCAP-OSSIv3.1]. Figure 157 illustrates the steps and communication paths
involved in collecting PNM data from a CM or CCAP device.




                                Figure 157 - Data Collection Sequence Diagram

The result of a successful PNM test and file upload is a PNM test result file that a PNM application can read and
analyze. By default, each PNM test result file name contains the PNM test, a device identifier such as the CM MAC



09/27/23                                     CableLabs                                                      169
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 170 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                      CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


address, and a timestamp. The PNM test file is a binary data file containing a header and a data section as defined in
[CM-OSSIv3.1] and [CCAP-OSSIv3.1].
For example, a successful downstream RxMER test against a CM with the MAC address 480033DDF724 and
executed on Friday, March 22, 2019, 8:30:57 AM GMT would generate a PNM test result file with the name
PNMDsMer_480033DDF724_1553243457. This file is fully configurable. By parsing the data contained in the file,
a chart similar to that shown in Figure 158 can be plotted. This figure shows the RxMER per subcarrier across a
DOCSIS 3.1 OFDM channel.




                          Figure 158 - Example of Downstream RxMER per Subcarrier


8.1.1    Operational Considerations
The use of TFTP by the DOCSIS 3.1 PNM Bulk-File Transfer mechanism may require some network configuration
changes if any firewalls are between the CM or CCAP and the destination TFTP server. TFTP is a UDP-based
protocol that uses ephemeral ports to transfer data. Figure 159 shows the upload sequence in which Ports X and Y
are ephemeral port numbers.




                                        Figure 159 - TFTP Upload Diagram


For the TFTP upload process to complete, any network firewalls between the CM or CCAP and the TFTP server
must be configured to allow UDP traffic from the ephemeral port range between the CM or CCAP and TFTP server
to pass. Though it may be possible to use source and destination addresses to configure an explicit rule for CCAP



09/27/23                                       CableLabs                                                         170
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 171 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                       CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


devices, it is not practical for CM subnets. As such, rules allowing UDP Port 69 and all UDP traffic in the ephemeral
port range destined for the TFTP server are required to support the PNM Bulk-File Transfer mechanism.

8.1.2    Security and Reliability Considerations
TFTP and SNMP are currently not specified to be encrypted. However, one can add application layer security if
desired. For example, HTTPS proxy can be added in front of data collectors like the Common Collection
Framework (CCF), which allows encrypted posts and gets.
UDP, which is low priority, is the assumed transport mechanism. As such, a capacity limit is reached if there is too
much traffic, and the UDP packets can be dropped, causing a loss of PNM information. Further, there is a general
assumption that if a test fails or if information is corrupted in the transfer process, then a retry of the test is
warranted. The application layer (in this case the PNM application) is assumed to manage the information reliability.
In addition, CMs can be limited in their processing of PNM information. If a PNM request takes 5 minutes to
complete and is only initiated when requested, then the CM will not likely respond to other requests until the first is
completed. Consider this when requesting data from CMs.

8.1.3    Deployment Considerations
The network needs to have a TFTP server to receive and hold the TFTP files that are responses to the data requests.
Also, firewall rules on the network must allow the TFTP packets to travel from the CM to the server and to any
applications that need the data.
Similarly, SNMP requires firewall rules to be configured to allow the traffic in both directions.
Virtual machines (VMs) are commonly used to deploy PNM data collectors such as CCF. The VM should have
enough cores and be configured properly for the deployment size. It is common to have one PNM data collector
instance for each CMTS or CCAP core, but different models are possible and should be considered based on
expected use.

8.1.4    IPDR Bulk Data Collection
Although the current DOCSIS specifications that cover PNM require SNMP and TFTP mechanisms for data
delivery, some cases require upstream data from the CCAP or CMTS be obtained via IPDR.
Spectrum data are also specified for SNMP by DOCSIS 3.0 technology, but billing data is in IPDR only. IPDR may
require a command-line interface to the CCAP or CMTS.

8.1.5    New Data Transfer Methods
Defined in 2021, there is a new bulk data transfer mechanism described in section 7.5 of [CCAP-OSSIv3.1]. This
mechanism is defined to better facilitate obtaining the increased amounts of disparate data from the CCAP or CMTS
resulting from PNM tests; it is also useful for other large data acquisition requirements. Based on the experience of
previous methods, a small group formed from the CableLabs PNM working group to define improved methods that
would allow more effective data transfer and thus more useful PNM testing involving the CCAP or CMTS.
In the configuration of a bulk data transfer, the destination for the resulting file is set, along with the transfer
protocol of choice: TFTP, HTTP, or HTTPS. When using TFTP, the CCAP or CMTS must have a TFTP client to
complete the file transfer, initiated by the CCAP or CMTS at the request of the PNM server. gNMI is also an option
specified, but it is not supported by DOCSIS 3.1. However, there is nothing preventing a CCAP or CMTS vendor
from offering it.
Though the working group is also moving toward streaming telemetry by using YANG in general, which will allow
significantly improved methods for data acquisition, the updated method discussed here can be implemented in
DOCSIS 3.1 through a software update.
For Remote PHY (R-PHY) networks, spectrum capture data is delivered to a PNM server over an L2TP pseudowire
in PSP format. While efficient, and a necessary difference due to the nature of R-PHY, vendors have not yet aligned
on how to configure data collection on remote PHY devices (RPDs).




09/27/23                                        CableLabs                                                          171
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 172 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                        CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


8.2        Upstream Priorities
As a refresher, DOCSIS 3.1 introduced a new set of requirements on the CM and CCAP related to the collection and
reporting of PNM data (see Section 9, "Proactive Network Maintenance" [PHYv3.1]). DOCSIS 3.1 introduced a
new bulk-data transfer mechanism that uses TFTP to upload PNM data to a destination TFTP server. Configuration
of the PNM test execution, file storage, and TFTP destination uses SNMP, as defined in [CM-OSSIv3.1] and
[CCAP-OSSIv3.1]. Figure 157 illustrates the steps and communication paths involved in collecting PNM data from
a CM or CCAP device.
The result of a successful PNM test and file upload is a PNM test result file that a PNM application can read and
analyze. By default, each PNM test result file name contains the PNM test, a device identifier such as the CCAP
identifier, and a timestamp. The PNM test file is a binary data file containing a header and a data section, as defined
in [CM-OSSIv3.1] and [CCAP-OSSIv3.1].
In the latest version of [CCAP-OSSIv3.1], the configuration table on the CCAP was changed based on the PNM-
WG recommendations to better support multiple applications, but the high-level mechanism of SNMP configuration
request followed by TFTP upload from the CCAP remains the same.
The following is a list of 2022 priorities in order. These are proposals and are subject to change if consensus within
the working group is achieved to re-order the following proposal.
      1.    Require support for TFTP upload of data for all PNM tests supported by the CCAP. This is already a
            MUST requirement in the CCAP OSSI specification but is not universally supported. Additionally, CCAPs
            should implement the latest bulk file transfer mechanism with support for TFTP, as specified in CCAP-
            OSSIv3.1-N-22.2232-1, or, alternatively, the previous CCAP bulk data transfer mechanism with support
            for a TFTP destination per PNM test.
      2.    Enable support for upstream triggered spectrum capture (UTSC) across all CMTS vendors. If the CMTS
            (vCMTS, R-MACPHY, RPD) supports DOCSIS 3.1 or higher, then it MUST include support for UTSC.
            a.   It is recommended that if a vendor does not currently support UTSC that they first implement the free-
                 running method of UTSC (i.e., crawl before you walk), then work to implement UTSC fully, as
                 specified.
            b.   It is recommended that if a vendor does not currently support UTSC that they first implement TFTP as
                 a method for sending UTSC data to the PNM server, then enable additional methods as specified for
                 the architecture (e.g., L2TP from the RPD).
            Note that, though UTSC is possible today for a number of CMTS hardware-software combinations, all
            known deployments require proprietary elements, and none follow the specification, so the industry
            generally does not have functional UTSC.
      3.    Enable the ability to obtain OFDMA MER (RxMER) from the CMTS in a quicker and more efficient
            method. The current mechanism for collecting OFDMA RxMER data is slow, as the data can only be
            requested for a single modem at a time for any OFDMA channel. For PNM and PMA applications that
            need to periodically collect the data for all cable modems, this serial request for each modem is slow and
            problematic. The requested enhancement would be to provide a mechanism to collect these data faster for
            all cable modems on the CCAP. Two suggested methods are described below.
            a.   Modify the existing definition of the docsPnmCmtsUsOfdmaRxMerCmMac to allow the use of a
                 wildcard or null MAC address. This would indicate to the CCAP to return RxMER data for matching
                 or all modems on the interface. The resulting uploaded measurement file would then contain a result
                 block for each modem.
            b.   Enable the CMTS to stream RxMER data over a Kafka bus.
      4.    Specifically for the above two items (and ultimately for all OSSI-related specifications), vendors should
            provide clear, consistent, accurate requests and responses across platforms.
            a.   This means that by following the specification and being able to demonstrate that as a vendor, one can
                 demonstrate they are following the specification.




09/27/23                                          CableLabs                                                        172
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 173 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                      CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


         b.   Vendors MUST follow the existing OSSI specification OR provide clear documentation of deviations
              from the specification.
              1.   Example 1: If vendor "A" configures UTSC using the majority of the UTSC specification but
                   deviates from the UTSC specification to require the end user to SSH into the CCAP and download
                   the capture file, it MUST be incumbent on the vendor to produce clear and concise documentation
                   on how and why the vendor has deviated from the specification. Further, the vendor MUST
                   provide documentation and code examples of how to use the "new" methods they have chosen to
                   adopt.
              2.   Example 2: If vendor "B" configures UTSC using a minority of the UTSC specification or a
                   proprietary implementation, it is required that the vendor provide a comprehensive guide on the
                   complete usage of their UTSC/proprietary implementation along with sample code in order to
                   expedite the implementation. In this case, vendor "B" must produce more documentation and code
                   because they have deviated further from the specification than vendor "A."
              3.   In either case of Example 1 or 2 above, both vendors MUST provide documentation and code
                   samples that represent the areas where they have deviated from the specification so that they can
                   demonstrate a working UTSC analyzer at CableLabs Interop events and so that third parties
                   (MSOs and vendors) can reproduce a working implementation of the UTSC analyzer using the
                   vendor CCAP and/or RPD.
              4.   Using parameters "A," "B," and "C" and returns parameters "X," "Y," and "Z" per the OSSI
                   specification, one would expect that when using any vendor’s platform, it should be possible to
                   configure UTSC using the same parameters "A," "B," and "C" and get the same return parameters
                   "X," "Y," and "Z."
         c.   Though amplitude values would be different, the configuration and expected output formatting should
              be identical and follow the OSSI specification.
         d.   Vendor consistency in reporting the format and units, regardless of the delivery mechanism, for both
              UTSC and RxMER, data must be consistent and well defined in the specification.

8.3     Measurement Calibration
Discussion of this topic in a future version of this document is being considered. For now, it is acknowledged that
calibration is an important factor when comparing measurements from different sources.

8.4     Using Complex Equalization and Channel Estimation Data
8.4.1    Applications
Until now, two main application categories were developed from analysis of the SC coefficients. The first was a
standalone path analysis. At a CM, this analysis revealed echo tunnels created by two or more impedance
mismatches along with other problems such as excessive group delay (GD), suck-outs, peaking, and tilt. The second
application was a matching path analysis, indicating that a group of CMs share a common impairment. This
information, along with the coaxial network topology, reveals likely locations of plant damage. Figure 160
illustrates upstream single carrier groupings done within a node, revealing homes with common impairments, which
are color coded on the map and spectral plots.




09/27/23                                       CableLabs                                                         173
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 174 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                    CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927




                  Figure 160 - Upstream SC Groupings Showing Common Plant Impairments

Applications for equalization data previously mentioned are single response analysis for impairments and matching
common impaired responses to assist in the location of line damage. The second application can be expanded to a
third application where matching nonimpaired (normal) responses are compared to determine if subscribers are
neighbors sharing a common cable segment. This can be done to validate records or as a source of information for
locating CMs in the network when location data are not available.
Another application for equalization data is for plant stability analysis. Intermittent connections will cause re-
adaption of coefficients to the new echoes. Connections may change infrequently, such as daily in the case of
thermal-caused intermittent problems, or several times per second if intermittent connections are experiencing wind
or traffic vibrations. Comparing responses with earlier stored coefficients reveals the presence of intermittent
connections. If the changes are large or frequent, the problem may be severe.
Equalization can also be affected by interference, such as band-limited ingress LTE signals or wider bandwidth
interference such as common path distortion (CPD) interference or passive inter-modulation (PIM). An examination
of the "smoothness" of an equalization solution can reveal noise causing a poor equalization setting. These data can
be complementary to MER-per-subcarrier data available on both OFDM and OFDMA. Figure 161 shows a lab
equalization response with a poor equalization solution in an LTE band.




      Figure 161 - LTE Interference Causing Poor Equalization Visible in the Right Pre-Equalization Plot


09/27/23                                      CableLabs                                                        174
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 175 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                      CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


Another application is determining if a selected cyclic prefix (CP) is sufficiently long to accommodate the longest
significant echoes that are being corrected. Figure 162 shows an example of the strength of echoes versus delay
time. The longer a signal travels in a cable, the greater the attenuation. Knowing the cable type allows this
attenuation vs. time curve to be corrected down to near the noise floor. Another application is determining if the
programmed CP length is long enough for the longest expected echo, which is shown in green in Figure 162. Any
echo longer than the CP must be sufficiently weak to cause no harm.




   Figure 162 - Impulse Response Visible in the Left Plot, Indicating Sufficient CP Allocation. There Are No
    Unexpected Echo Tunnels as Peaks Above the Line and No Echoes Over the Green Corner Threshold.
                            Red Limit of 20.6 dB per Microsecond Added to Plot


Existing applications of impaired response matching and individual response matching work better with multicarrier
than single carrier because the equalization data have more dynamic range, in part because of using elevated pilots.
The wider bandwidth of the signals also provides for more accurate time resolution.
Possible applications include the following:
    1.   wideband downstream and upstream response matching;
    2.   investigation of correct sizing of OFDM(A) CP length;
    3.   intermittent detection from unstable equalization responses;
    4.   interference or ingress causing band-limited wrong equalization values;
    5.   in-house wiring problems, such as broken coaxial shields, identified by energy in low index coefficients;
    6.   water detection in coaxial cable;
    7.   locating subscribers connected to the same coaxial line by matching responses or identifying amplifiers in a
         cascade; and
    8.   field test gear, including time domain reflectometer (TDR) functionality.
Experience from using PNM objects has shown that operational value is gained by learning what physical problems
are manifested in observed channel responses. There is an opportunity to now collect appropriate feedback from
cable operators to understand what problems create unusual sets of complex equalizer coefficients for the much
wider bandwidth OFDM and OFDMA channels.
An operational disservice occurs when time and resources are wasted on chasing non-problems. An impairment that
is time-variable in nature is more suspicious than one that is static. Likewise, an impairment that increases with time,
such as water slowly entering a cable, should be flagged for inspection and possible repair.



09/27/23                                        CableLabs                                                         175
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 176 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                      CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


DOCSIS equalization is powerful, and many impairments are masked as a result. For example, if a tap's return loss
drops from a specified 18 dB to 15 dB, the equalizers can normally compensate so no service impairment is
experienced by the customer.

8.4.2    Methods for Processing Complex Data
In DOCSIS 3.1 specifications, both OFDM and OFDMA were standardized. Pre-distortion for upstream OFDMA
carriers was retained, although it was made optional. On both modulation types, MIBs were specified to reveal the
frequency domain (FD) complex coefficients associated with each of the potentially thousands of active subcarriers.
A MIB object request is sent to the CM, and the CM performs a trivial file transfer protocol (TFTP) transfer of a file
containing the requested in-phase (I) and quadrature (Q) coefficients. Note that the upstream coefficients returned
are for the corrections to remove impairments, not for the impairments themselves. In the downstream, the CM can
report channel estimation coefficients for OFDM, which are also complex coefficients.
With both upstream and downstream DOCSIS 3.0 single carrier (SC) signals, the returned complex coefficients are
in the time domain (TD). Alternately, the returned coefficients in the multi-carrier (MC) systems, both OFDM and
OFDMA, are FD coefficients. The discrete fast Fourier transform (FFT) and inverse fast Fourier transform (IFFT)
may be used to convert between time and frequency domains.
Raw complex (I,Q) data from CMs often have an added uniform time delay, which appears as a phase rotation in the
frequency domain, and a direct current component, both of which are introduced by the CM-CMTS interaction and
not as components of the plant response. Therefore, to see the actual plant response, which is needed to understand
impairment issues, it is important to remove the DC term and the added delay. The DC term is apparent in the
frequency response, and it is usually a safe assumption to use the apparent peak as the DC response as though it is a
known signal (like a pilot subcarrier) when calibrating the complex data.
In this method, there is a coarse delay removal step done in the frequency domain followed by a phase rotation done
in the time domain. The phase rotation is done to make the DC term real-only, so the imaginary component of the
DC term is forced to zero. In other words, the DC "pilot" is calibrated. With this assumption, the remaining signal
reflects the plant condition, which allows us to process these data for our described use cases.
At a high level, the steps are as follows.
    1.   Import the complex FD data.
    2.   Estimate linear delay (dφ/dω)—calculate ω using arc tangent to get phase angle.
    3.   Remove the coarse delay—flatten the angle vs. frequency plot for the middle portion of the band. Using
         only the middle portion of the band prevents group delay (GD) from effecting the calculation.
    4.   Convert corrected response to time domain using IFFT, and measure phase on the DC term.
    5.   Correct phase on all time samples so Im[0]=0 by a time-domain rotation.
    6.   View response in FD by a FFT calculation.
    7.   Optionally, normalize coefficients. For example, make the total power in all coefficients equal to unity, or
         make the DC term 1.0.
    8.   (Optional) Compare with another response using FD division.
These steps are further explained in an SCTE Expo paper from 2021: "OFDMA Predistortion Coefficient and
OFDM Channel Estimation Decoding and Analysis" [Williams et al.].




09/27/23                                       CableLabs                                                         176
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 177 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                              CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


9 PNM PRACTICES
9.1        Measures of Access Network Health
This section provides a starting point for measuring access network health, which can be used as a performance
measure for network operations, and potentially as a measure for selecting maintenance to prioritize.
There are two related concepts considered in this section: the quality of downstream (and upstream) plant, and the
quality of downstream (and upstream) service with respect to the plant condition. This section first focuses on
downstream measures, but also mentions briefly how to address this question for the upstream too.
General guidelines in summary are as follows.
      •     Quality of downstream plant—Start with RxMER per subcarrier, translate to bit loading, and use RxMER
            stats to assess when spectral efficiency is a concern. This is currently accessed on the CM via the
            CmDsOfdmRxMer MIB. But to access the actual bit loading, the table CmDsOfdmModProfile is used. A
            new measurement, it provides modulation profile by subcarrier. CM firmware is being updated as of this
            publication date, so this capability may become more common soon. There should be considerations for
            input signal level and FEC stats as well. Too low or high of a signal level can indicate problems, and very
            high correctable codewords or any uncorrectable codewords can indicate vulnerability to smaller
            impairments. To determine outliers, the values need to be compared to expectations, which should come
            from design guidelines, and may be specific for each operator.
      •     Quality of downstream service with respect to downstream plant—Use RxMER margin for the given
            profile for the CM, then sum the margin over all subcarriers. This is currently managed with the
            CmDsOfdmMerMargin MIB. CmDsOfdmModProfile data may be of use for this measurement too.
            Considerations should be given to the service provided to the customer too. More specifically, a low
            latency service may not tolerate retries as much as typical internet service.
      •     Averages over any period of time consisting of at least 10 samples, using statistical average or
            exponentially weighted moving average, for a stable measure. This would be managed outside the CM by
            the cable operator retaining the measures above over time and then assessing the data over that period to
            determine patterns and impacts over the period. Operators typically sample PNM statistics each hour,
            though some sample less frequently still. This is mostly a factor of the maturity and capability of the data
            collection systems available to the operators.
      •     Averages over any population or random sample from a population of CMs using statistical average to get
            estimates for the plant section. Samples should include at least 50% of the population for populations under
            100, and at least the maximum of 20% or 50 CMs for larger populations (assumed, should be tested, should
            be sufficient). This item would be managed outside the CM by the cable operator retaining the measures
            above over time and then assessing the data over that period to determine patterns and impacts over the
            period. These measures should generally be collected over a given node.

9.1.1       Quality of Downstream Plant (for Each OFDM Signal)

9.1.1.1      RxMER per Subcarrier Approach
      1.    Retrieve RxMER per subcarrier data (say once per hour or every two hours) using the CmDsOfdmRxMer
            MIB.
      2.    Calculate the bit loading appropriate for each subcarrier, applying any operating margin desired. 16 Use the
            CmDsOfdmRequiredQamMer object to determine the RxMER target values required in each subcarrier,
            then determine the appropriate bit loading by comparing the RxMER values to the target values, per
            subcarrier. If docsPnmCmDsOfdmMerMarTable is not available, the default values from the specification
            are provided below. (Note that, when available, this can be done (or confirmed) using
            CmDsOfdmModProfile table.)


16 See section I.3 of this technical report which discusses FEC and RxMER per subcarrier test results and guidelines for operators

for more information on this subject.


09/27/23                                            CableLabs                                                               177
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 178 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                             CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


    3.    Translate into maximum symbol size if and as desired.
    4.    Use any of these three as the measure of downstream plant quality.
The bit loading recommendations from the current OSSI specification [CM-OSSIv3.1], and the similar table of
CMTS Minimum CNR performance in AWGN channel form, are shown in the tables below, though operators may
choose to adjust these recommendations to their own needs.

      example, ReqMerQAM128 reports a value of 96 in ¼ dB, which is equivalent to 96/4 =24 dB.

                   64-QAM                       23.0                        -4 dBmV                        0 dB
                   128-QAM                      26.0                        0 dBmV                         0 dB
                   256-QAM                      29.0                        0 dBmV                         0 dB
                   512-QAM                      32.5                        0 dBmV                         0 dB
                  1024-QAM                      35.5                        0 dBmV                         0 dB
                  2048-QAM                      39.0                        7 dBmV                         0 dB
                  4096-QAM                      43.0                        10 dBmV                        0 dB
            Note 1. CNR is defined here as the ratio of average signal power in occupied bandwidth to the average
              noise power in the occupied bandwidth given by the noise power spectral density integrated over the
              same occupied bandwidth.
            Note 2. Channel CNR is adjusted to the required level by measuring the source inband noise including
              phase noise component and adding the required delta noise from an external AWGN generator.
            Note 3. The channel CNR requirements are for OFDMA channels with non-boosted pilots. For operation with
              boosted pilots, which is optional at the CMTS, the CNR requirements are increased by
              a) 1 dB for channels with 50 kHz subcarrier spacing, and
              b) 0.5 dB for channels with 25 kHz subcarrier spacing. For example, the CNR requirement for QPSK with
              boosted pilots is 12.0 dB with 50 kHz subcarrier spacing and 11.5 dB with 25 kHz subcarrier spacing.



9.1.1.2     RxMER Stats Approach
    1.    Poll RxMER stats at a suitable rate using CmDsOfdmRxMer MIB.


09/27/23                                           CableLabs                                                              178
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 179 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                        CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


    2.    Identify any outlying values of 2nd percentile or 5th percentile statistics, and poll RxMER per subcarrier,
          then use the RxMER per subcarrier approach above to find their quality measure.
    3.    For those RxMER stats with nth percentile values not far from the mean, apply a uniform bit loading to
          estimate the bit loading possible on the whole channel.
    4.    Translate into a maximum symbol size as desired.
    5.    Use any of the three results as measures of downstream plant quality, recognizing that those based on
          RxMER stats are approximations.
The nodes with outlier 2nd percentile or 5th percentile values can be quickly identified for closer examination. The
standard deviation can also provide further insight into where to focus maintenance.

9.1.2     Quality of Downstream Service with Respect to Downstream Plant
This section provides only a view of current practices with a view of general service health. Much more work should
be done for consideration of specific services and use cases, including impact on latency, real time use cases, and
other specific use case considerations. The general guidance in this section serves as a base and starting point for
more development by operators who choose to differentiate. But what is provided her is informative toward
maintenance decisions, which is an ultimate goal of making service health actionable.
Certainly, timeouts and sub-optimal profile assignment are key indicators of service health problems. With the ease
of access to PNM measurements like profile, RxMER per subcarrier, and FEC stats, more can be done after timeout
issues are discovered.

9.1.2.1    RxMER Margin and Profiles for Downstream Health
For downstream service quality, the profile in use is important. The profile in use can be compared to the service
offered, and judgement about the suitability of the profile for providing the level of service may be rendered.
    1.    Select the desired profile, preferably the current or most likely used profile for the given CM.
    2.    Poll RxMER margin at a suitable interval of time using CmDsOfdmMerMargin MIB, providing the desired
          threshold offset value (ThreshldOffset).
    3.    Use either the number of subcarriers below threshold as the measure of quality, or repeat with a more
          suitable profile if NumSubcarriersBelowThrshld is greater than 0.
A possible replacement for network health indication for correctable error when using OFDM channels is the
number of iterations it takes to correct all of the errored bits in an LDPC codeword. However, this is not available
today. Our studies comparing FEC statistics to RxMER, which is used to assign downstream profiles, reveals the
complex relationship between these measurements. In Section 9.3 of this document, those test results and guidelines
for the use of FEC stats for maintenance decisions are provided.

9.1.3     Consideration of FEC for Upstream Health
RxMER statistics are not easily obtainable in the upstream today. But when available, they can be used in a similar
way as described for the downstream. FEC statistics are not generally available in the upstream.
A possible replacement for network health indication for correctable error when using OFDM or OFDMA channels
is the number of iterations it takes to correct all of the errored bits in an LDPC codeword.
Low Density Parity Coding (LDPC) is used for the inner Forward Error Correction (FEC) of both DOCSIS OFDM
and OFDMA channels. LDPC differs from traditional Reed-Solomon (RS) Encoding in that it uses much larger code
words spread across all of the channel.
Traditionally FEC errors, both corrected and uncorrected, have been used as an indication of network health. With
the introduction of DOCSIS 3.1 OFDMA and OFDMA channels, a more aggressive (powerful) FEC scheme is used,
specifically LDPC. With LDPC coding it is normal to have corrected errors on a healthy channel. It can even be




09/27/23                                         CableLabs                                                             179
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 180 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                             CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


argued that if there are no FEC errors that the channel is being underutilized. Seeing uncorrectable errors is still an
issue, as it indicates packet errors and loss. 17
LDPC decoders are iterative, if a codeword is not successfully decoded on first pass, a second pass is performed.
The number of decoding passes continues to increase until either the codeword is successfully decode, or the
codeword is declared uncorrectable. The number of passes is implementation dependent.
Currently the only way to observe the number of iterations is in the trailer of UEPI packets sent from RPDs. These
trailers include a count of the average number of iterations for each LDPC codeword size. This is defined in Table 8
of the [R-UEPI] specification. It is possible to capture UEPI packets and monitor these counts on a per channel
basis. The UEPI interface is used for Remote PHY connections to RPDs.

9.1.4    About Averages
For a single CM sampled over a time period, make the samples as evenly spaced as possible over the time period,
but also account for any known cyclical issues such as temperature, time of day, weather, etc. For example, for a
single day quality estimate, sample at least 10 times over the day, which may be every two hours for convenience,
for a total of 12 samples. For a monthly sample, collect over random parts of the day for every other day of the
month.
For a group of CMs, an average of results taken over a population or sample from a population for approximately
the same time interval is best. For a point in time, sample the CMs as closely as possible to that time. For an interval
of time, collect samples over the time to cover it well; anything larger than a few hours will require multiple samples
from each CM (see above).
Averaging can be done over time using either the statistical average (sample sum over sample size) or an
exponentially weighted moving average to weigh current values higher than past values, providing a measure that
might better account for random fluctuations yet be a current representation of quality.

9.1.5    Sampling Frequency for Intermittent Problems
Intermittent problems are well known to be difficult to capture. If it is assumed that intermittent issues are
essentially random, or that there is no knowledge about when they generally occur, then using observations of
variations through periodic sampling much catch them.
If intermittent impairments arrive randomly at E events per day with an expected duration of D seconds per event,
then the proportion of the day that an intermittent event is happening is
Pevent = E (events/day) * D (seconds/ event) / (24*60*60 (seconds/day))
Likewise, to capture these events with a probability Pcapture, then it takes n samples of the data to capture it as
         Pcapture = 1-(1-Pevent)n
Solving for n, results are
         n = ln(1-Pcapture)/ln(1-Pevent)
where ln is the natural log.
To find the impairment with the given probability Pcapture over say m days, then it is sampled at a rate of S = n/m per
day. Also, finding the minutes between samples is
         I = 24*60/S.
The table below shows a few example results.




17 Further CRC check at Ethernet MAC level is required to determine which packets, if any, are errored.

                                        Table 12 - Calculate Sample Frequency

                 E              D             Pcapture             m               S                  I
             Imp. Freq.      Imp. Dur.      Prob. Catch        Scan Dur.       Scan Freq.       Scan interval
              event/day       second           0<x<1              day          sample/day        min/sample
