Note that some of these scenarios requires frequent sampling, which is not possible for some measurements such as
RxMER per subcarrier, or full spectrum capture.

9.1.6    Stability
If these health indicators are not stable over time, then that lack of stability can also be an indication of poor network
or service health or both. There are several methods for adding variability in measurements to a health indicator.
    •    A measurement range over time is a good indication of variability or lack of stability and can be weighted
         into the measurement itself.
    •    Using weighted averages of the measurements, so that poor values are weighted more heavily or for a
         longer period of time, can also make intermittent poor values weight nearly as much or as much as
         consistently poor values, as desired.
         •    For more heavily weighted values, consider a quadradic loss function, such as the square of the
              difference from the value to the ideal value.
         •    For weighing values over time, consider an exponentially weighted moving average or window
              average with a weighted function of the values in the average.

9.1.7    Impairments
Consideration of impairments and their severity is worthwhile as well. See Section 9.2 on the network maintenance
decision process and the discussion of impairment severity for a severity-based approach worthy of consideration.
Note also that the paper "Profile Management Informed Proactive Network Maintenance" from SCTE Expo 2020
[PMI PNM] provides a comparison of several methods of network and service health for selecting maintenance.
Also, there are recent SCTE papers published which apply machine learning techniques to detect anomalies
(impairments) in RxMER per subcarrier or spectrum data: [Zhu et al.], [Ferreira et al.].

9.1.8    Conclusions
In the downstream, RxMER per subcarrier measurements are a great indicator of network and service health. FEC
statistics can be a contributor to health indication too, particularly in the upstream; but FEC errors in the downstream
indicate a health problem beyond what profiles are addressing, and RxMER statistics can indicate.




09/27/23                                        CableLabs                                                           181
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 182 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                        CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


When these health measures are used to identify proactive work, this section provides high-level starting positions
for that work. Additional features to be considered are outlined in Section 9.2, "Network Maintenance Decision
Process."
Other measures such as T3 and T4 timeouts may be indicators as well but are not proactive in nature; T3 and T4
timeouts may indicate noticeable service degradation which should be addressed through maintenance. This section
focuses on the problem of prioritizing proactive maintenance work, so focus is just on those indicators that inform
that problem. FEC statistics and RxMER in combination work very well as a starting point for this. But as found in
[PMI PNM], profiles can have a significant impact on the selection of proactive work, though clearly profiles are not
the best indicator of network or service health when RxMER and FEC statistics are available.

9.2       Network Maintenance Decision Process
The network maintenance tools often referred to as PNM tools are useful for all types of network maintenance,
proactive and otherwise. It is not helpful to differentiate between proactive and reactive maintenance, especially
when making use of maintenance tools. Many of the methods outlined in this report work for all types of
maintenance use cases.
There are many identifiable elements that contribute to the decision to do maintenance, be that proactive or reactive.
      •    Problem identification—that it exists, and location—If a customer calls in the problem, then you have an
           easy place to start. But that can be misleading too. If the problem is broader, the problem may not be near
           that customer, so additional work may be needed to isolate the problem, and certainly to identify it. If using
           network information to identify a problem, then you are actually on your way to identifying the issue faster
           than a customer call.
      •    Significance of impact—If a customer calls in the problem, you have a good view to at least part of the
           problem. Others may be impacted, and additional impact may not be felt immediately at or before the
           customer calls. If using network information to gage significance, you may be able to quantify the impact
           in more uniform terms.
      •    Sufficiency of information to address the problem—When a customer calls, you know you must address
           the service issue. But if using network data to determine a network issue that may not yet be a service issue,
           then an important part of the decision process is whether or not enough information is available to make the
           problem addressable.
      •    Cost effectiveness to address the problem—Sometimes opportunities, like a mandatory reactive repair, will
           lower the cost of a proactive repair, making it an imperative. When considering the degradation and further
           damage that is possible, some proactive tools can allow operators to get ahead of costly repairs.
      •    Resources available to address the problem—When ahead of the problem, operators can plan and schedule
           maintenance when resources are available and less expensive.
      •    Success probable—In learning how to associate network data with service and network conditions, data
           can be collected and tracked, increasing the likelihood of successful resolution. This will inform planning
           and decision making for maintenance.
      •    Priority for operations—With all the information outlined above, operators are equipped to prioritize work
           and optimize operations spend.
The distinction between proactive and reactive maintenance is subtle, and not so cleanly separate.
Operators who are fully occupied with reactive maintenance can still get ahead of the work to be proactive
defendants of service. By embracing some of the newer network operations tools which are based on network data
that are available through network elements already, they can troubleshoot more efficiently and effectively. Once
operations becomes more efficient, and resources are freed up, then those resources can be more focused on the
network data that foretells of network problems. Eventually, operations falls down the slippery slope of proactive
network maintenance simply by being efficient.
By using the proactive tools, sometimes in reactive ways, operators will slip into doing more proactive work simply
because it is more efficient and better for service to do so. There really is no hurdle to get over; simply mix in



09/27/23                                         CableLabs                                                          182
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 183 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                        CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


proactive when and where it makes sense to do, and you will be over any perceived hurdle. By using PNM tools
effectively, for reactive problems too, operators find that reactive repairs can be streamlined, freeing resources for
proactive work. Mixing proactive with reactive increases the opportunity density, reducing wasted truck rolling time
and increasing overall operations efficiency.
Keep in mind that there are network problems that DOCSIS will work around, and thus hide if you aren't using
proactive tools to look. These do not necessarily impact service but can contribute to smaller issues causing service
impact that otherwise would not and may indicate network damage that must be addressed. Some network damage
puts service at risk, and some will get worse and more expensive if left alone. Changes in performance
measurements can indicate which problems are new versus which are getting worse versus which are stable.
Note also that the paper [PMI PNM] from SCTE Expo 2020 provides a comparison of several methods of network
and service health for selecting maintenance. From that paper, the following conclusions can be used:
      "Profile management has a clear influence on the impact to service that results from PNM. Therefore, some
      consideration of profiles is important when selecting proactive work to do. It is advisable that operators at least
      look at the impact on profiles when determining whether to conduct PNM, and it is likely a factor in prioritizing
      proactive work.
      Anomaly-driven PNM, finding and eliminating anomalies, appears to be the most popular approach that
      operators take for their PNM; but modem-driven PNM is an option to consider for some, as the data that most
      informs PNM comes from the CMs. Likewise, profile-driven PNM is an option to consider as well. But
      ultimately, each of these factors needs to be considered in some way in deciding which PNM work to take on.
      Though this paper does not compare all options, an examination of the options presented here does suggest that
      impairment-driven identification of maintenance opportunities is a very good approach, that problem severity
      can be measured by impact on CMs, and that it is necessary and complimentary to PNM decision making to
      consider the impact on profiles when determining the capacity impact on the network for selecting maintenance.
      Said differently—find anomalies, investigate their impact on customers' service, estimate the impact due to
      profiles and on network impact expected from the work, and use these pieces of information to prioritize and
      select PNM opportunities. It may at times be best to let profiles drive PNM maintenance decisions, in fact.
      Seeing that some network repairs can have negative impact on profiles, it suggests more work on profiles is
      warranted too. It may be simple enough to set a rule that says, if all CMs improve, then the recalculation of
      profiles can only result in a net benefit, or a benefit to each CM, or another appropriate rule, before
      consideration of replacing profiles. In other words, do not harm first. Alternately, it may be useful to develop
      algorithms that set profiles to be robust against these changes. Likewise, it may be worth investigating different
      objectives when setting profiles altogether."
A methodology and framework for building and testing various maintenance decision processes is available from
CableLabs and is explained in [Rupe and Zhu].

9.3     FEC, MER and Decoding in the Downstream
Cable operators have for many years tracked pre-FEC and post-FEC bit errors in legacy SC-QAM based digital
video and DOCSIS networks. Those legacy SC-QAM signals, based on ITU-T J.83, use concatenated coding with
outer-code Reed-Solomon forward error correction. The onset of pre-FEC bit errors for the Reed-Solomon decoder
has long been used by many operators to determine that performance degradation is starting to occur, and that the
cause of the pre-FEC errors should be identified and fixed before post-FEC bit errors occur. Post-FEC bit errors
result in packet loss.
DOCSIS 3.1 introduced a more powerful FEC to the cable industry, known as low density parity check (LDPC).
Because of the way LDPC works, it is not possible to rely upon the presence of correctable codeword errors to
indicate the onset of potential problems. Indeed, one can have 100% correctable codeword errors and expect
generally reliable modem operation, with no real indication of how close to the digital cliff one is. What to do?
One proposed possible solution is to track RxMER per subcarrier (relative to the RxMER threshold for the
modulation order in use) along with correctable and uncorrectable codeword errors. The PNM Working Group
commissioned CableLabs to do this testing, and the test results are in Appendix II.




09/27/23                                         CableLabs                                                          183
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 184 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                              CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


Some plots in the test results of that appendix show FEC decoder input, which is the sum of the correctable
codewords and the uncorrectable codewords as a percentage of total codewords. These are the codewords that failed
pre-decoder syndrome check. So, one minus the FEC decoder input is the percent of error free codewords after
slicing and prior to decoding. It is easiest to tell which data in the figures are from FEC decoder input as they stay
flat (and nearly 100%) at below-threshold MER values while the underlying uncorrectable codeword percent
increases as MER decreases from above-threshold to below-threshold.
Total codewords is the sum of the number of error-free, correctable, and uncorrectable codewords. FEC decoder
input is the sum of the correctable codewords and the uncorrectable codewords. All are reported as a percentage of
total codewords.
Note on codeword error terminology: The DOCSIS 3.1 downstream FEC includes LDPC and BCH, while the
upstream uses only LDPC. In the downstream, the BCH can be thought of as being analogous to a broom and
dustpan, sweeping up residual errors, and providing the ability to get correctable and uncorrectable codeword error
counts. Among the available downstream FEC statistics are: uncorrectable codewords (the number of codewords
that failed BCH decoding); correctable codewords (the number of codewords that failed pre-decoding LDPC
syndrome check and passed BCH decoding); and the total number of FEC codewords.
In the upstream, because there is only LDPC, one cannot get the same FEC statistics that are available in the
downstream. Among the available upstream FEC statistics are: pre-FEC error-free codewords (the number of
codewords that passed pre-decoding syndrome check); unreliable codewords (the number of codewords that failed
post-decoding syndrome check); corrected codewords (the number of codewords that failed pre-decoding syndrome
check, but passed post-decoding syndrome check); and the total number of FEC codewords.
Replicated with permission, from R. Prodan, L. Montreuil, A Kliger, B. Shen, "Demystifying the DOCSIS 3.1
PHY," SCTE Cable-Tec EXPO, September 22-25, 2014, [Prodan et al.], see the following two unlabeled figures and
explanatory text which supports these results.
"DOCSIS 3.1 employs several forward error correction codes for various purposes. This section will discuss both
the downstream and upstream forward error correction codes for protecting the payload user data. Several relevant
characteristics for these FEC codes are as follows:
Downstream Code:
     •    DVB-C2 18 (16200,14400) inner LDPC + (14400,14232) outer BCH 19
     •    Overall Rate 0.8785
Upstream Codes:
     •    (16200,14400) Quasi-Cyclic LDPC Rate 0.8889
     •    (5940,5040) Quasi-Cyclic LDPC Rate 0.8485
     •    (1120,840) Quasi-Cyclic LDPC Rate 0.75
The bit error ratio performance as a function of SNR for the downstream FEC code for all square, non-square, and
mixed modulation modes from 64-QAM to 4096-QAM is shown in the figure below. The 10-8 bit error ratio SNR
threshold for each modulation order is tabulated from this graph. The spectral efficiency is given by the code rate
times the number of bits per QAM subcarrier for a given modulation order. The resultant spectral efficiency
(bits/s/Hz) vs. SNR is shown in figure 16. 20 The Shannon capacity limit is also shown in this figure to evaluate the
relative proximity of the performance of the downstream FEC code. The figure shows this FEC code to be about 3
dB from the Shannon limit or about one bit/s/Hz below the Shannon limit."

From [Sundaresen et al.], Section 3, see results that align with these test results where there is overlap, and the work
in our new paper confirms and extends the work in this paper. While our work tested a wider range of conditions
and CMs, this referenced paper covered the case of profile switchover. If profiles are allowed to switch in a given
implementation, the adjustments may continue to keep service stable (avoid uncorrectable codeword errors), though
at a cost of data rate. But if profiles are not allowed to be switched, then the uncorrectable codeword errors may
impact service.
Outer BCH coding leads to definitive determination of errored codewords, unlike LDPC which only indicates error
free assurance. LDPC alone leaves open whether or not codewords not absolutely error-free are errored.
LDPC is used for the inner FEC of both DOCSIS OFDM and OFDMA channels. LDPC differs from traditional
Reed-Solomon (RS) Encoding in that it uses much larger code words spread across all of the channel.

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 187 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                        CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


Traditionally FEC errors, both corrected and uncorrected, have been used as an indication of network health. With
the introduction of DOCSIS 3.1 OFDMA and OFDMA channels, a more aggressive FEC scheme is used,
specifically LDPC. With LDPC coding it is normal to have corrected errors on a healthy channel. It can even be
argued that if there are no FEC errors that the channel is being underutilized. Seeing uncorrectable errors is still an
issue, as it indicates packet loss.
LDPC decoders are iterative: if a codeword is not successfully decoded on first pass, a second pass is performed.
The number of decoding passes continues to increase until either the codeword is successfully decode, or the
codeword is declared uncorrectable. The number of passes is implementation dependent.

Appendix I              Anatomy of a Suckout
I.1 Introduction
When characterizing the frequency response of a cable network's radio frequency (RF) spectrum using broadband
sweep or similar (see [Hranac Freq Resp]), a variety of impairments can be identified. One frequency response
impairment of interest is what is commonly called a suckout, 21 a type of notch in the frequency response, which can
have a variety of causes.
Figure 163 (from [Hranac, Campbell, et al.]) shows a full band capture (FBC) screen shot of the downstream
spectrum. A suckout is visible as an approximately 20 dB deep vee-shaped notch centered just above 700 MHz.




            Figure 163 - Full-Band Capture Screen Capture Showing Suckout at Just Above 700 MHz
                                          (graphic courtesy of Akleza)

Before continuing the discussion on suckouts, it is important to understand some of the related parameters and
concepts.

I.2 What Is Frequency Response?
Frequency response is an important parameter that has long been used to characterize the performance of a cable
network. The following definitions are from cable industry references and may help to clarify what is meant by
frequency response.
                                                               ***
          From [Grant]: "The transmission loss or gain of a system, device, or network measured across the
          transmission bandwidth presented, as compared with the loss or gain at some reference frequency
          within that bandwidth."
                                                         ***



21 The term "suckout" has long been used to describe a notch in the frequency response of a cable network’s downstream or

upstream RF spectrum (or individual channel) like that shown in Figure 165. Interestingly, "suckout" is not a cable industry-
exclusive term. The audio field uses it to describe, for example, a notch in the measured audio frequency response produced by
loudspeakers. "Suckout" also is sometimes mistakenly used to describe what happens when hardline cable pulls out of a
connector during extremely cold temperatures (the cable's physical length contracts, causing the end of the cable to pull out of the
connector; "pull-out" is more accurate in this context).


09/27/23                                            CableLabs                                                                 188

       From[Ciciora et al.]: "The gain as a function of frequency through a system or device, relative to
       the defined ideal. In the case of a cable distribution system, this is also known as peak-to-valley
       response, or P/V."
                                                          ***
       From [Hranac Freq Resp]: "One of several metrics that can be used to determine the performance
       of a component, device, system, or network. The term frequency response is more accurately
       called complex frequency response, the latter a measure of magnitude- and phase-versus-
       frequency. According to Wikipedia,
           'Frequency response is the quantitative measure of the output spectrum of a system or
           device in response to a stimulus and is used to characterize the dynamics of the system. It is
           a measure of magnitude and phase of the output as a function of frequency, in comparison
           to the input.'
       In cable industry vernacular, frequency response usually refers to just magnitude (amplitude)-
       versus-frequency and is a measure of the overall gain variation of a cable network or an individual
       channel as a function of frequency.

       continuous wave (CW) carrier of a certain amplitude at the input to a device under test (DUT), as

       constant amplitude) across a frequency range of interest for the DUT. The output of the DUT is
       measured and the results plotted graphically, similar to what is shown in Figure 165.

I.3 Frequency Response Impairments
As mentioned earlier, broadband sweep and similar can show a variety of frequency response impairments.
Figure 166, from [Spaulding], shows several common examples of cable network frequency response impairments,
plus an example of noise or ingress interference, that can be seen in FBC screen shots. The vertical axis in each plot
is magnitude or amplitude, and the horizontal axis is frequency.




      Figure 166 - Examples of Common Cable Network Frequency Response Impairments and Ingress


I.3.1 Standing Waves

When a transmission line of a given impedance is connected to a load or termination that has the same impedance,
an incident wave traveling through that transmission line will be completely absorbed by the load; however, when
the load's impedance is not the same as the transmission line's, an impedance mismatch exists, and some or all of the
incident wave is reflected back toward the source. The interaction of the incident wave and reflected wave produces
a distribution of fields in the transmission line known as a standing wave. More specifically, the constructive and
destructive interference of forward and reflected waves produces a superposition of the two waves in the
transmission line. For more information about standing waves, see [Gray], [Smith], and [SCTE 270].
The coaxial cable portions of cable networks are designed and built to have a nominal impedance of 75 ohms (Ω).
Practically speaking, it is not possible for the impedances of passive and active devices, connectors, and even the
cable itself to be exactly 75 Ω at all frequencies, so there will always be impedance mismatches that cause



09/27/23                                       CableLabs                                                         190
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 191 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                              CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


reflections (echoes). Under normal operating conditions, the reflections have a small enough magnitude to not be of
concern.
When impedance mismatches are moderate to severe, the echoes they cause can impact the frequency response in
detectable ways. They can cause amplitude ripple in the frequency domain, which may affect signal quality.

I.3.2 Amplitude Ripple

Amplitude ripple is commonly (but incorrectly) called a standing wave. Technically speaking, standing waves cause
amplitude ripple, which has a periodic scalloped or sinusoidal-like shape in the frequency response. 22
NOTE: Amplitude ripple is a contributor to frequency response amplitude variation, which includes effects such as
      tilt, roll-off, notches, and suckouts (discussed later), and non-periodic phenomena such as "water waves"
      caused by water in the coaxial cable or other components.
As shown in Figure 167 and Figure 168, amplitude ripple comprises a series of evenly spaced crests (peaks) and
troughs (valleys). Figure 167 shows an example of amplitude ripple with a sinusoidal-like shape, and Figure 168
shows an example that illustrates a scalloped shape.
NOTE: The troughs (valleys) in the amplitude ripple visible in both figures are technically not notches or suckouts.
Figure 167 shows an FBC example with sinusoidal-like amplitude ripple in the cable network's downstream
frequency response. The frequency spacing between adjacent crests (or adjacent troughs) is about 54 MHz.




                   Figure 167 - FBC Example Showing Sinusoidal-Like Amplitude Ripple in the
                 Cable Network's Downstream Frequency Response (graphic courtesy of Akleza)




22 The square of the magnitude (voltage) response, the power spectral density for example, displayed without taking the logarithm

(that is, a linear scale in power, not a logarithmic scale in “dB”), can be shown mathematically to appear as a pure sinusoid.
When displaying in dB, a large ripple will appear with the scalloped shape, though when displayed on a linear scale, it appears as
a sinusoid. Unless otherwise noted, the frequency response displays in this document use a logarithmic vertical scale.


09/27/23                                            CableLabs                                                               191
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 192 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                       CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927




         Figure 168 - FBC Example (from [Hranac, Campbell, et al.]) Showing a Scalloped Shape in the
                        Downstream Frequency Response (graphic courtesy of Akleza)

Knowing the frequency spacing between adjacent crests or adjacent troughs can help determine the length of an
echo tunnel or echo cavity. An echo tunnel is the span of coaxial cable between two impedance mismatches that
results in amplitude ripple in the frequency response. One end of the echo tunnel might be an amplifier or similar,
and the other end a damaged device such as a corroded tap. The formula (from [SCTE 270]) to calculate the length
of an echo tunnel is
                                                                      𝑉𝑉𝑉𝑉
                                                     𝐷𝐷 = 492 ∗ �            �
                                                                    𝑓𝑓𝑀𝑀𝑀𝑀𝑀𝑀
where
    D is distance or length in feet,
    VF is the coaxial cable's velocity factor, and
    fMHz is the frequency separation in megahertz between the amplitude ripple's adjacent crests (peaks) or adjacent
    troughs (valleys).
For the amplitude ripple shown in Figure 167, the length of the echo tunnel is about 7.9 feet, assuming the cable's
velocity factor is 0.87.

I.3.3 Notch

A notch is a frequency response characteristic in which a single frequency or narrow band of adjacent frequencies is
attenuated intentionally or unintentionally, while other frequencies are relatively unaffected.
A frequency response notch can be intentional; for example, the result of a notch filter, channel deletion filter, or
similar (see Figure 170, Figure 169, Figure 170, Figure 171, Figure 172, and Figure 173).
Figure 169 shows an image of a cylindrical device, which is a commercial filter. This form factor has been used for
decades by the cable industry. Depending on the make and/or model, the filter can be a notch filter, bandstop or
channel deletion filter, high-pass filter, etc.




09/27/23                                        CableLabs                                                              192
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 193 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                   CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927




                           Figure 169 - Commercial Filter (photo courtesy of Akleza)




    Figure 170 - Narrow Bandwidth Notch Filter Frequency Response (graphic courtesy of Arcom Digital)


Figure 171 shows an FBC screen capture of the response of a commercial single-channel notch filter, centered on
CTA channel 74 (522 MHz to 528 MHz). Figure 172 shows a closer look at the same notch.

<!-- PDF_PAGE: 194 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                  CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927




        Figure 171 - FBC Screen Capture Showing the Response of a Single-Channel Notch Filter on
                               CTA Channel 74 (graphic courtesy of Akleza)




             Figure 172 - Close-up of the Same Notch in Figure 171 (graphic courtesy of Akleza)


Figure 173 shows an FBC screen capture showing the wide bandwidth notch caused by a bandstop filter. In this
example, about 40 channels have been attenuated by the filter.




09/27/23                                     CableLabs                                                        194
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 195 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                      CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927




 Figure 173 - FBC Screen Capture Showing the Wide Bandwidth Notch Caused by a Bandstop Filter (graphic
                                          courtesy of Akleza)


In years past, some cable operators used what were known as negative traps for conditional access. The trap was
usually installed at the tap port to prevent a subscriber from receiving one or more premium channels. To receive the
channel, the trap had to be removed. A single-channel negative trap would produce a roughly 6 MHz-wide notch in
the spectrum of the subscriber drop (the actual bandwidth of the notch varied somewhat depending on filter design
but usually removed at least the visual carrier and sometimes most of the channel).
Positive traps were also used for conditional access, but the function was opposite that of a negative trap. In this
case, the positive trap had to be installed in order for the subscriber to receive a premium channel. The positive trap
was a high-Q factor (see Section I.4) narrowband notch filter that removed a pair of closely spaced "jamming"
carriers intentionally inserted between the visual and aural carriers of the channel. The presence of the carriers made
it difficult or impossible for a TV set to receive the channel.
A notch can also be unintentional, typically caused by a plant impairment. Such a notch is often called a "suckout"
(see Section I.3.4).
An example of an apparent notch that really should not be called a notch is when a channel slot in the spectrum is
intentionally left vacant. When viewed on a spectrum analyzer or similar (usually configured to display a wide
frequency range), the unoccupied channel can resemble a notch. Refer to Figure 167, which shows a vacant channel
slot between 450 MHz and 500 MHz, and, coincidentally, near one of the amplitude ripple's troughs.
In summary, a notch is the result of a filter-like effect—intentional or otherwise—in the network. Absence of
modulation energy or power does not constitute a notch.

I.3.4 Suckout

As mentioned previously, a suckout is a type of notch, usually (but not always) of relatively narrow bandwidth.
Figure 163 shows an example of a suckout; Figure 174 (from [Hranac, Campbell, et al.]) shows an example of a
severe suckout.
A frequency response suckout is almost always unintentional. In this instance, some kind of fault in the cable
network is the culprit, often inside of an active or passive device housing. Typical causes include loose modules,
module covers, and printed circuit boards; cold solder joints; poor module grounding; and similar problems.
A suckout can also be caused by periodic impedance discontinuities in coaxial cable. Such impedance
discontinuities can degrade the cable's structural return loss (SRL) on a frequency (or frequencies) typically related
to a half-wavelength or integer multiples of a half-wavelength spacing if the periodic impedance discontinuities
[Lubars], [ANSI/SCTE 03 2016].




09/27/23                                        CableLabs                                                           195
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 196 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                                   CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927




                                   Figure 174 - FBC Example Showing a Severe Suckout


As mentioned previously, periodic impedance discontinuities in coaxial cable can cause suckouts. Figure 175 shows
an example of what has been called the "mold spike suckout," occurring in the first generation of MC2 hardline
cable. A periodic impedance discontinuity affecting the cable's SRL between 500 MHz and 600 MHz was caused by
the physical dimensions of the mold used to place groups of disks on the center conductor during manufacture. The
mold was redesigned for the second generation of that cable, moving the SRL spike and resulting frequency
response suckout above 1 GHz. While these coaxial cables are no longer available, they are still in use in some cable
networks.




         Figure 175 - Sweep Display Showing So-Called Mold Spike Suckout Located at About 550 MHz


I.4 Quality Factor
One can describe a notch (or suckout) in the frequency response by a parameter called quality factor, or Q factor. 23
Assuming the suckout in Figure 163 has a center frequency of about 715 MHz and a -3 dB bandwidth of about
50 MHz (about eight SC-QAM channels wide), the approximate Q factor = 715 MHz/50 MHz = 14.3.
To help clarify the concept of Q factor when describing a notch (or suckout), visualize three different suckouts
centered at 715 MHz. If the first suckout's -3 dB bandwidth is 100 MHz, its Q factor = 715 MHz/100 MHz = 7.15. If
the second suckout's -3 dB bandwidth is 50 MHz, its Q factor is the previously mentioned 14.3. Finally, if the
suckout's -3 dB bandwidth is 25 MHz, its Q factor is 715 MHz/25 MHz = 28.6.



23 From Wikipedia: "In physics and engineering, the quality factor or Q factor is a dimensionless parameter that describes how

underdamped an oscillator or resonator is. It is defined as the ratio of the initial energy stored in the resonator to the energy lost in
one radian of the cycle of oscillation. Q factor is alternatively defined as the ratio of a resonator's centre frequency to its
bandwidth when subject to an oscillating driving force. These two definitions give numerically similar, but not identical, results.
Higher Q indicates a lower rate of energy loss and the oscillations die out more slowly."


09/27/23                                              CableLabs                                                                    196
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 197 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                         CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


The suckout in Figure 174 has an approximate -3 dB bandwidth of 210 MHz and a center frequency of about
560 MHz. Its Q factor is 560 MHz/210 MHz = 2.67.
Note that Q factor is helpful when comparing different suckouts at a given frequency but does not fairly compare
them at different frequencies.

I.5 How to Create a Simple Notch Filter
One could purchase a commercially manufactured notch filter to demonstrate what a notch looks like (refer to
Figure 170). It is also possible to create frequency response notches with homebrew approaches, such as a
quarter-wavelength coaxial cable stub or a circuit made using a resistor, inductor, and capacitor.

I.5.1 λ/4 Coaxial Stub

It is possible to create a similar frequency response notch as that shown in Figure 163 with an open-circuit
quarter-wavelength (λ/4) coaxial stub connected to a transmission line using a simple tee (see Figure 176).
NOTE: An open circuit λ/4 coaxial stub creates a notch at the fundamental frequency and notches at odd multiples
      of the fundamental frequency. For example, if an open-circuit λ/4 stub is cut to length for 60 MHz, the stub
      will produce notches in the frequency response at 60 MHz, 180 MHz, 300 MHz, 420 MHz, etc.




         Figure 176 - Open-Circuit Quarter-Wavelength Coaxial Stub Connected to Transmission Line
                                             with Tee Adapter


What is the impedance of a λ/4 coaxial stub?
At first glance, one might be inclined to think that an open-circuit λ/4 coaxial stub made from Series 6 drop cable
has the same 75 Ω nominal impedance of the coaxial cable or, when connected to a tee, perhaps 75 Ω/2 = 37.5 Ω.
However, it is not quite that simple. An open-circuit λ/4 transmission line has a load impedance Zload = ∞ because
of that open circuit. An interesting property of a λ/4 transmission line is impedance transformation, which, in this
case, converts the load impedance Zload = ∞ at the end of the stub to Zin = 0 as "seen" at the stub's input, creating a
short circuit at the stub's resonant frequency. In other words, there is little energy at that frequency and its odd
harmonics transmitted in the transmission line beyond the stub—hence, we have a notch filter. For more information
about the impedance transformation property of a length of transmission line (see [Gray]).
Assuming the use of Series 6 coaxial cable (velocity factor = 0.85) for the transmission line and stub, the length of a
λ/4 stub for 715 MHz can be calculated with the following formula (from [SCTE 270]):
                                        𝜆𝜆⁄4𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖ℎ𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒 = (2950.713⁄𝑓𝑓𝑀𝑀𝑀𝑀𝑀𝑀 ) ∗ 𝑉𝑉𝑉𝑉
                                        𝜆𝜆⁄4𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖ℎ𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒 = (2950.713⁄715) ∗ 0.85
                                                   𝜆𝜆⁄4𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖𝑖ℎ𝑒𝑒𝑒𝑒 = 3.51




09/27/23                                        CableLabs                                                         197
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 198 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                   CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


Figure 177 shows an example of an open-circuit λ/4 stub cut to length for 715 MHz.




                               Figure 177 - Open-Circuit λ/4 Stub Cut for 715 MHz

installed, and Figure 179 shows the FBC response after the stub was installed. In addition to the notch at 715 MHz,
this stub will also produce notches at odd multiples of 715 MHz (2.145 GHz, 3.575 GHz, etc.), which are not shown
in the FBC display.

I.5.2 Series RLC Circuit

One could also use a circuit comprising a resistor (R), inductor (L), and capacitor (C) connected in series (called a
series RLC circuit) to create a single-frequency notch filter.
From https://resources.system-analysis.cadence.com/blog/msa2021-understanding-a-notch-filter-transfer-function:
         A series RLC circuit is often used as a simple type of bandpass filter. When the output is taken
         across the resistor, the circuit will function as a bandpass filter. In contrast, taking the output
         voltage across the L and C elements together gives notch filter behavior. Notch filter behavior is
         related to resonance in that, at resonance, the L and C elements give equal and opposite voltage
         drops, leading to 0 V output from the filter circuit.
Refer to Figure 181, which shows a series RLC circuit used as a notch filter (taken from https://resources.system-
analysis.cadence.com/blog/msa2021-understanding-a-notch-filter-transfer-function).




09/27/23                                        CableLabs                                                         199
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 200 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                            CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927




                                Figure 181 - Series RLC Circuit Used as a Notch Filter


A series RLC circuit that is resonant at 715 MHz could have the following values(see Figure 182, which shows an
RLC circuit example (taken from https://en.wikiversity.org/wiki/RLC_circuit). 24 Formulas for determining the
component values of a series RLC circuit are easily found online or in the literature, but the tool on the referenced
website helps to make component selection a bit easier and allows some experimentation with component values.




                                           Figure 182 - RLC Circuit Example

      stages—to achieve higher Q factor performance.

I.6 Conclusion
As mentioned in the introduction, characterization of a cable network's frequency response can identify a variety of
impairments. Of particular interest is a frequency response suckout, which is a type of notch, and is almost always
unintentional. Typical causes include loose modules, module covers, and printed circuit boards; cold solder joints;
poor module grounding; and similar problems. A suckout can also be caused by periodic impedance discontinuities
in coaxial cable. An example of the latter is the so-called mold spike suckout in MC2 hardline cable.
Troughs in a display of amplitude ripple are not suckouts, though widely spaced troughs (in the frequency domain)
caused by a very short time delay micro-reflection might be mistaken for (and even called) suckouts.
The importance of identifying different frequency response impairments cannot be overemphasized, whether done
using broadband sweep, full-band capture from cable modems, or other methods. Degraded frequency response,
including suckouts, can have a detrimental impact on network performance. Understanding different kinds of
impairments can help operators efficiently locate and repair the causes of those impairments.




09/27/23                                       CableLabs                                                        202
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 203 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                              CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


Appendix II Error Correction Margin Solution Analysis
II.1 Introduction
Cable operators have for many years tracked pre-FEC and post-FEC bit errors in legacy SC-QAM based digital
video and DOCSIS networks. Those legacy SC-QAM signals, based on ITU-T J.83, use concatenated coding with
outer-code Reed-Solomon forward error correction. The onset of pre-FEC bit errors for the Reed-Solomon decoder
has long been used by many operators to determine that performance degradation is starting to occur, and that the
cause of the pre-FEC errors should be identified and fixed before post-FEC bit errors occur. Post-FEC bit errors
result in packet loss.
DOCSIS 3.1 introduced a more powerful FEC to the cable industry, known as low density parity check (LDPC).
Because of the way LDPC works, it is not possible to rely upon the presence of correctable codeword errors to
indicate the onset of potential problems. Indeed, one can have 100% correctable codeword errors and expect
generally reliable modem operation, with no real indication of how close to the digital cliff one is. What to do?
One proposed possible solution is to track RxMER per subcarrier (relative to the RxMER threshold for the
modulation order in use) along with correctable and uncorrectable codeword errors.
There is interest in DOCSIS 3.1 OFDM RxMER per subcarrier values at which correctable and uncorrectable
codeword errors begin to appear. This is with and without profile changes. There is also interest in the triggering of
a profile change as an indicator. Finally, operators want to confirm the effectiveness of a nominal 6 dB of operating
headroom above the RxMER threshold for the modulation order in use, and identify risks with operation at lower
headroom values.
Several controlled tests were conducted at CableLabs, with the test procedures and results summarized in this
document.

II.2 Error Correction Margin Test Plan
II.2.1 Test Set A

Test set up 1 is similar to section 3.3 of [Sundaresen et al.], an SCTE PMA paper. An Agilent/Keysight N5182A
MXG Vector Signal Generator (VSG) with AWGN option as an AWGN source was used.
A test plant was set up with at least two manufacturers of each chip set (total four CM manufacturers) and at least
two CMs of each copy for a total of at least eight CMs, connected to a splitter setup. That splitter was fed by a
nearby OFDM source, to avoid the need to use an external amplifier.
First, the RxMER responses for these CMs and test set up to get consistent levels (flat input) were calibrated.
Then configured test conditions were configured so the CMs get equal received power levels. There were four sets
of tests: one set each at +10 dBmV, 0 dBmV, -6 dBmV, and -10 dBmV RF input to the modems.
For this first set of tests, all (static) profiles were fixed to 4096-QAM. 25 In most cases, where not indicated, a
192-MHz-wide channel was used.
The remaining procedures were as follows. Obtain a baseline reported RxMER per subcarrier from the DOCSIS 3.1
modems (data collected via TFTP from the CmDsOfdmRxMer object) when the CNR is in the high 40s (ideally
there should be no correctable codeword errors reported) at the desired RF input level(s). Using a Keysight UXA,
measure OFDM RxMER per subcarrier too. 26


25 It was desired to put together a test set-up that has high enough CNR to be above the point where correctable codeword errors

show up. For instance, if testing 4096-QAM, that would require a CNR in the high 40s.
Keep in mind that RxMER per subcarrier values much above the mid 40s (as reported by a cable modem) may be suspect. For
such high RxMER values, some significant portion of the measured noise may be contributed by the CM or UXA, and as such
may not be a precise indication of the input to the measuring device.
26 In figures and text showing results, sometimes the Keysight UXA measurements are described more generally as VSA for

vector signal analyzer.


09/27/23                                            CableLabs                                                               203
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 204 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                          CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


Introduce noise level uniformly on all frequencies first. Start injecting wideband AWGN (enough to cover the entire
OFDM signal spectrum), increasing its amplitude such that the carrier-to-noise ratio (CNR) at the modems is able to
be reduced in 1 dB steps during the tests (smaller increments as the limits were reached). At each step record
RxMER per subcarrier, and FEC stats (uncorrected, correctable, and uncorrectable codewords). Uncorrected
codewords are codewords that were determined to contain no errors even prior to decoding. Look for the onset of
correctable codeword errors. Increase the noise level (decrease the CNR) until uncorrectable codeword errors reach
50%, or if not possible then at least until correctable codeword errors decline for at least three increases are seen 27.
As CNR is reduced in 1 dB steps, correctable codeword errors will eventually show up. Note the value at each 1 dB
drop in CNR. Repeat until the onset of uncorrectable codeword errors. Keep dropping CNR until the OFDM signal
no longer passes any data at all.
Test set up 2: repeat with an attenuator value to achieve 0 dBmV RF input.
Test set up 3: repeat with an attenuator value to achieve -6 dBmV RF input.
Test set up 4: repeat with an attenuator value to achieve -10 dBmV RF input.

II.2.2 Test Set B

Repeat test set A but with controlled (simulated) LTE ingress instead of AWGN.

II.2.3 Test Set C

Repeat test with AWGN configured to produce a CNR near (about 3 dB above) the RxMER (SNR) threshold (as
reported in specifications and repeated later in this section). Then add LTE ingress, and increase the strength of the
LTE signal until failure.

Some plots in the test results show FEC decoder input, which is the sum of the correctable codewords and the
uncorrectable codewords as a percentage of total codewords. These are the codewords that failed pre-decoder
syndrome check. So, one minus the FEC decoder input is the percent of error free codewords after slicing and prior
to decoding. It is easiest to tell which data in the figures are from FEC decoder input as they stay flat (and nearly

27
   We might find that we reach 100% correctable codeword errors fairly quickly, where they remain until uncorrectable
codeword errors start to show up. When within about 6 dB of the RxMER threshold for a given modulation order, without ingress
or other severe impairment, the correctable codeword errors are near or at 100% all the way down to the RxMER threshold.


09/27/23                                          CableLabs                                                            204
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 205 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                      CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


100%) at below-threshold MER values while the underlying uncorrectable codeword percent increases as MER
decreases from above-threshold to below-threshold.
Total codewords is the sum of the number of error-free, correctable, and uncorrectable codewords. FEC decoder
input is the sum of the correctable codewords and the uncorrectable codewords. All are reported as a percentage of
total codewords.

II.3 Relating FEC to RxMER in OFDM for Avoiding Uncorrectable Codeword Errors
II.3.1 AWGN Flat Noise Only

In the following plots, the MER(dB) values are derived from ratios of signal power and error power at the OFDM
demodulator's slicer for each subcarrier, for both the CM and UXA measurements. The MER for these curves
correspond to the average RxMER (averaging the "dB" values of each subcarrier RxMER value in "dB"); because
the test condition is intended as a flat channel with flat noise (AWGN) there is some, but minimal, variation in the
RxMER per subcarriers. Further, some plots show FEC decoder input, which is the sum of the correctable
codewords and the uncorrectable codewords as a percentage of all codewords.




                             Figure 186 - CM and VSA Input Signal Power -10 dBmV




09/27/23                                       CableLabs                                                         205
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 206 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)        CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927




                        Figure 187 - CM and VSA Input Signal Power -6 dBmV




                        Figure 188 - CM and VSA Input Signal Power 0 dBmV




09/27/23                              CableLabs                                           206
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 207 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                      CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927




                            Figure 189 - CM and VSA Input Signal Power +10 dBmV


Refer to Figure 186 through Figure 189. Note that the figures show FEC decoder input, which is the sum total of all
correctable and uncorrectable codewords. Also recall the test configuration that the UXA (which is our vector signal
analyzer (VSA)) is connected at the same time in the same setup with the CMs, so both measurements are taken at
the same time, but resulting in different dB values.
Also note that the UXA measures RxMER using data subcarriers instead of pilots as the CM uses.
    •   CM and UXA measurements do not agree on the average RxMER values. CMs seem to perform with no
        uncorrectable codeword errors (100% correct codewords) down to a level just below 36 dB RxMER on
        their own measure, whereas the UXA reports a higher RxMER value corresponding to the FEC threshold.
    •   CMs differ somewhat in the RxMER level at which they take on errors (correctable and uncorrectable),
        though in this set of tested CMs the difference is not very large.
    •   There is at least a 6 dB range between where (a) the CM sends nearly 100% of the data through the
        decoders and (b) the CM has no uncorrectable codeword errors after decoding, resulting in nearly 100%
        correctable codeword errors.
    •   As uncorrectable codeword errors appear, increasing the AWGN slightly to reduce the CNR by less than
        0.5 dB, the CM loses connection (sync) with the CMTS.
    •   These results suggest that,
        •    As long as the (average) RxMER is above 36 dB (for 4096-QAM) the CM will perform fine under flat
             AWGN.
        •    If a given plant has low variability in its noise level, operators could expect service to remain reliable
             much closer to the 36 dB limit, so could set an operating headroom threshold at a lower RxMER value
             than if a plant has high variability with noise. In other words, while more reliable operation is expected
             when RxMER is 6 dB or more above the threshold, one could operate with less headroom if the plant
             is stable and free of ingress.




09/27/23                                       CableLabs                                                          207
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 208 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                         CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


    •      Issues to be studied include
           •     What if noise is focused over a subset of frequencies (such as with ingress).
           •     Are there differences in these results for different modulation orders, or OFDM channel sizes?
           •     Does LTE have a different impact than random noise over the same frequencies.
To address these issues to be studied, bin noise (such as narrow band ingress) to the test will be introduced.
As power level seems not to matter with these tests, only testing at 0 dBmV signal power level in subsequent tests
will be done.

                             Reporting Average RxMER Over the Whole Channel


If profiles are fixed, new or intermittent ingress or spurious impairments will cause problems sooner than if profiles
can adjust to compensate for the presence of impairments, or profiles are set to be robust against known
(intermittent) impairments.
Seeing a drop in a profile can indicate that a problem has occurred, and there may be a reason to investigate.
But lacking a drop in the profile, and looking at average RxMER, there is only about 2 dB of room between taking
on correctable codeword errors and having a significant number or even full uncorrectable codeword errors (making
the CM lose connection and the data not showing in the plot). This observation suggests that any time a CM is
experiencing correctable codeword errors it may be vulnerable to LTE or other ingress or channel noise. If an
operator is not able to manage a tight plant, it may be important to manage profiles conservatively, or keep a large
margin in RxMER.

Figure 197 (above) shows different values of noise floor, and increasing the LTE level to lower RxMER values in
the LTE frequency range; this time the results are plotted against average RxMER over the signal, not just the LTE.
It does not seem to matter how much noise floor there is; the LTE level dominates.
At 100% correctable codeword errors you are vulnerable (about 2 dB loss overall and uncorrectable codeword errors
could begin). Therefore, again, when considering issues like in-channel ingress, to the extent it is possible, you do
not want to have CMs operating with correctable codeword errors.
However, it may be helpful to start looking at correctable codewords, then examining the RxMER per subcarrier
data for "notch" noise, and measuring the RxMER values in the notch, to determine how close the CM is to taking
on uncorrectable codeword errors.

II.4 Conclusions
It is normal and expected behavior to see 100% correctable codeword errors in the downstream when the RxMER
per subcarrier is within about 6 dB of the RxMER threshold for the modulation order in use. Good engineering
practice suggests maintaining at least 6 dB of operating headroom above the RxMER threshold for reliable
operation. Following this guidance, the minimum recommended operating RxMER for 4096-QAM is about 41~42
dB, which is about 6 dB above the approximately 35~36 dB threshold for that modulation order (see the graphs in
the Addendums section). As long as the cable network remains stable and impairment-free—and there are no
uncorrectable codeword errors—the correctable codeword errors can be ignored.
Some cable operators choose a smaller amount of headroom, as low as 2 dB or 3 dB, above the RxMER threshold.
While this may be satisfactory in a stable and impairment-free plant, the presence of impairments can increase the
risk of degraded performance.
Observations from the lab testing suggest that, while CMs can indeed operate reliably within about 6 dB of the
RxMER threshold, the risk of problems increases when in-channel spurious noise and interference are present.
Degraded performance may be more likely to occur, such that the issue(s) should be addressed proactively. Even a
small bit of ingress could severely impact service. At the very least, if a CM is experiencing any amount of
correctable codeword errors, and there is evidence of ingress or similar in-channel interference, even though small, it
should be addressed.
In fact, these results suggest that any ingress should be addressed in a cost-effective way.
Further, it is best to have an OFDM signal wide enough that any expected amount of LTE or other interference does
not cause CMs to lose connection. For instance, when the bandwidth of LTE ingress exceeds approximately 5% of
the bandwidth of the OFDM signal, CM performance is more likely to be impacted.
In all cases, some form of profile management is recommended, whether built-in to the CMTS or CCAP or part of a
third-party or standalone tool. With profiles that can tailor to noise issues in some bands, further resiliency can be
had, and an imperfect profile can also indicate an issue in the plant that should be addressed before disrupting
service too. The combination of profile management and sufficient operating headroom will ensure the most reliable
overall OFDM performance.




09/27/23                                        CableLabs                                                        215
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 216 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                    CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


Appendix III Lab Test Results for Determining Water Present in Drop Cable
A drop from the field was obtained so that the existence of water in the drop could be visually confirmed. A new
drop using the same cable length and type was then created for comparison in the tests that follow.
For another indication of the existence of water in a coax cable, see the S12 measurements for a normal unimpaired
section of coaxial cable (Figure 198) versus the same type of cable that has been affected by water (Figure 199).
Note the loss as a function of frequency is rather smooth in an unimpaired cable, but it is not smooth at all in the
case of a wet cable. For a treatment of S-parameters including S12, see the PNM point-of-view document on Full
Duplex DOCSIS® or Ron Hranac's Broadband Library article on the subject (https://broadbandlibrary.com/a-quick-
look-at-s-parameters/).

Next, the group delay of the wet cable was examined, as shown in Figure 200. Note that the group delay is uneven to
a small degree over the measurable frequencies. At about 1500 MHz, measurement is not possible, so the plot shows
much higher variability. Ignoring the portion of the plot that is noise and not measurable above 1500 MHz, the plot
shows that group delay variation appears in wet cables.

                                          Delay from 50 MHz to 1200 MHz




                               Figure 200 - Group Delay for a Water-Soaked Cable


A time domain reflectometer (TDR) was applied to a pair of cables, one wet (WWI cable) and the other in good
health, both the same type and length of cable (Series 6, 100 ft). Figure 201 shows the S11 and S22 values that are
reflection coefficients. More energy is reflected in the wet cable compared to the dry cable. Note that the claim is
not that the entire length of the wet cable is filled with water; there is no way to measure how much of the cable is
water soaked and to what degree. The data only show that some amount of water in the cable will appear differently
in reflection measurements (S11 and S22).




              Figure 201 - S11 and S22 Measurements for a Water-Soaked Cable (WWI Cable) and
              a Clean, Unimpaired, Dry Cable of the Same Length and Type (100 Feet, Series 6)


Next, the same two cables' S11 group delay values are compared, shown in Figure 202. Note again that the wet cable
shows variability over the measurable frequencies before about 1.5 GHz (1500 MHz), and there is a lot of noise over
the higher frequencies that are not reliably measurable. However, the unimpaired cable shows nearly flat across the
entire plot, at both lower and higher frequencies. Clearly, wet cable has reflective properties and impacts group
delay as well.




09/27/23                                       CableLabs                                                        217
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 218 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                         CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927

                a Clean, Unimpaired, Dry Cable of the Same Length and Type (100 Feet, Series 6)


Looking at the magnitude S-parameter values for these two cables, shown in Figure 203, there are several
differences.
    •    Comparing S11 values, the wet cable's values (blue) are higher and slightly more variable than the values
         for the dry cable (orange).
    •    Comparing S12 values, the wet cable's values (green) are highly variable and lower than the very stable and
         higher values for the dry cable (straight red line).
    •    Comparing S21 values, the wet cable's values (purple) again are highly variable and lower than the very
         stable and higher values for the dry cable (straight brown line).
    •    Comparing the S22 values, the wet cable's values (pink) are higher and a bit less variable than the values for
         the dry cable (gray).
Although any of the S-parameters can be used to differentiate a wet cable from a normal dry cable, S12 and S21
appear to show the difference most clearly.
Note: a cable was not tested with a standing wave for comparison. A follow-up step would be to include other types
of cable impairments to see if S-parameters can be used to differentiate between different types of impairments.
However, because the S-parameters can technically define whether a cable is impaired or not, the exercise would be
only to determine if S-parameters can be used to differentiate between different types of impairments.
The primary concern with this test is to find data that can be utilized to differentiate between a standing wave related
to an impedance mismatch and one indicating a wet cable. Both are impairments, but a wet cable may be more
difficult to spot visually in the field, though it is likely isolated to a small span of hard line or a single drop and may
be easy to isolate using FBC in CMs.

Appendix IV Failure Modes, Effects, and Criticality Analysis
A failure modes, effects, and criticality analysis (FMECA) is useful for understanding how components can fail,
how those failures propagate through the network or system up to services, which failure modes are most severe,
and which causes should be addressed directly. By extending FMECA to include faults that impact service but do
not lead to service failure, we can identify failure modes that lead to faults and then determine how to best identify,
localize, and eliminate them. This is the main goal of PNM.

IV.1         Access Network Components
The access network, depicted in Figure 204, comprises subsystems and components, including the headend out to
the customer premise and all parts in between. It includes fiber and coax, including connectors, filters, combiners,
amplifiers, remote devices, cable modems, line and drop passives, set-top boxes, and power supplies. It may include
analog or digital fiber equipment. A complete list of the components identified is provided in the FMECA tables in
this section, acknowledging that we may not have a complete list of all components possible. For the sake of PNM,
our focus is on the access network as depicted in Figure 204, with acknowledgement to the external forces, systems,
and use cases that impact RF faults that lead to PNM opportunities.




                                Figure 204 - Block Diagram of the Access Network


IV.2         Failure Modes of the Access Network
The failure modes of the access network are numerous, with similar failure modes existing across the network, and
many related in terms of cause, effects, and how to localize. As a result, troubleshooting and fault management of
coax networks is difficult and can be time consuming.
General categories of coax network faults and categories of causes are shown in Table 15. Note that degradation
failure modes are prominent, and these are best detected and mitigated through PNM. They are caused by
environment issues, but insufficient or incorrect materials can be more vulnerable and accelerate degradation, and
people can cause damage or install improperly, which also accelerates degradation (when degradation is possible).
Damage failure modes are linked to degradation because damage can accelerate degradation. Damage itself can be
caused by people, such as colliding with a pedestal; the environment, such as wind and lightning; tools that can, for
example, cut the insulation on a drop; and material that is not sufficient to withstand damage events. Incorrect
installation can also lead to faults and can be caused by people, the procedures they follow, the tools they use, the
material the network is made of, or the environment. However, before that, incorrect or insufficient design can result
in a network that performs poorly and experiences faults caused by poor design, poor execution of design by people




09/27/23                                        CableLabs                                                          219
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 220 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                     CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


(including manufacturing defects or packaging or shipping problems), or poor procedures for following design. Note
how the failure modes and causes go deep into the network and also far before and beyond it.

                          Table 15 - Generalized Failure Mode Types with Causes in Common

          Failure Mode Type               Cause 1        Cause 2         Cause 3         Cause 4         Cause 5
  Degradation
                                        Environment   Material        People
  (worse with time, variable)
  Damage
  (compounds with events, abrupt, may   People        Environment     Tools            Material
  be stable or variable)
  Incorrect install
  (stable, abrupt, may show stable or   People        Procedure       Tools            Material        Environment
  variable measurements depending)
  Incorrect/insufficient design
                                        Design        People          Procedure
  (systemic mix of issues)


The full FMECA is not easy to reproduce in a document such as this, but some of the more important components
that have been completed rather fully at this point are shown in Table 16, Table 17, and Table 18. Note that
additional work is warranted to identify all effects, clarify their impact on RF signals, and assign approximate
probabilities as part of the criticality portion of the FMECA. However, the work done so far tells us a lot about how
to conduct effective PNM and what research is needed for the future.

IV.3         Effects of Failure Modes on RF, Network, and Service
Each failure mode may have a particular effect on the network components and RF signals (labeled sub-effect in
Table 19), the overall network (network effect), and services provided over the network (service effect).
For some failure modes, before the RF is impacted, network components begin to degrade. The effect of some
failure modes then is to only invite or accelerate degradation of the components. Degradation effects are coded in
green. Eventually, some failure modes degrade enough to introduce variability in network performance or RF; those
are written in purple in the tables. Some failure modes have an impact on RF, either directly or when degradation is
severe; those effects are written in orange. Finally, some failure modes are catastrophic in impact, such as a cut
cable, power failure, or failed active component; those are written in red. Because some failure modes can have
different severity, the effects indicated may or may not happen in all cases and may or may not be immediately
detectable from these effects.
Table 19 shows the failure mode types the PNM-WG has identified from the failure modes and their effects. The
effects relate to the failure mode types; for example, degradation failure modes are categorized this way because
they lead to or accelerate degradation, whereas damage failure modes have immediate impact and may also
accelerate degradation. Note that type D refers to degradation or damage, whereas type I refers to inadequate design
or installation.
To add clarity to the connection between the effects and how we detect them generally, see Table 18. Here we note
the methods for detecting the failure modes by effect. We also indicate which failure modes are linked to
degradation or damage and which are linked to insufficient installation or design.

PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                       CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927


 Table 19 - How the Effects of Failure Modes are Detected; for Type(s), D Refers to Degradation or Damage,
                              and I Refers to Insufficient Design or Installation




IV.4           Causes and Criticality
Table 19 also shows the causes as they relate to the failure mode categories. Rather than assign causes to each
specific failure mode, we identified cause categories for each failure mode type for ease of understanding and to
make the results more applicable. Certainly, some operators may have specific causes they identify for important
failure modes that are unique to them and should be addressed directly. But, generally, the causes of certain types of
failure modes are the same, regardless of where in the network they apply, so, for efficiency, we chose this
approach.
For degradation and damage failure modes, see Table 20; here we show some of the specific types and causes, as
well as contributing factors.

Criticality is addressed in the typical way in this analysis—as a multiplication or similar functional combination of
    •     the number of customers impacted, which is linked to the location in the network, as well as the failure
          mode;
    •     the duration of the impact, which is linked to the service provider's network operations; and
    •     the severity of the impact, which is linked strongly to the effect.

IV.5            Degradation Models
Degradation in the outside plant, and sometimes even internal components, happens due to corrosion or intrusion of
unwanted elements—it gets complicated. We have a few ways of describing our current state of knowledge for how
degradation behaves.
Figure 205 and Figure 206 show the life of a section of coax as it degrades, suffers some small damage events, and
is left to completely degrade to failure. The horizontal lines represent the layers of the coax. A blue line shows the
degradation getting worse over time; as degradation worsens, it cuts through the layers moving down to the
conductor, and time progresses to the right. Likewise, a red line shows the penetration of the water and damage, as
might be indicated in RxMER per subcarrier, pre-equalization, or spectrum capture. Note that the blue line cannot
move up toward improvement, but the red line can because it can dry, or movement can cease, for some amount of
time. Also note that shovel damage or rodent chew can lead to a rapid shock event that accelerates the degradation.
While Figure 205 and Figure 206 show everything that was just described, there is additional information in
Figure 206; each red block is annotated to describe the event it covers, and a plot of FEC statistics (correctable and
uncorrectable codewords) is overlaid to depict possible impact to service over these states.




09/27/23                                          CableLabs                                                         224
<!-- PDF_PAGE: 225 -->
PNM Current Methods and Practices in HFC Networks (DOCSIS® 3.1)                     CM-GL-PNM-3.1-V05-230927




                   Figure 205 - Degradation of a Coax Cable Left to Degrade Fully Over Time




  Figure 206 - The Same Depiction as in Figure 205 but with Additional Information of State Changes in the
                         Coax and Expected Impact on FEC Statistics Over Time


Figure 207 depicts in more detail the degradation states that service experiences as the network degrades. The gray
to black boxes describe the degradation of service, whereas the yellow callout boxes annotate what might be
detectable from PNM data while in these states. State changes are indicated with arrows pointing in the direction of
movement; red arrows indicate degradation in service, and green arrows indicate improvement by changes in
condition or repair.
Figure 208 explains graphically how various fault and failure causes can combine into spectrum impairments (RF
faults), which indicate the severity of problems in the network. While a network may have one failure mode that
must be addressed, it is more common that multiple impairments combine to impact service, leading to the need to
be addressed through PNM.

                        Corrects, with Descriptions of Service Impact and Detection
